Quantum Hoare Logic with Ghost Variables
Dominique Unruh

Abstract
Quantum Hoare logic allows us to reason about quantum programs. We present an extension of quantum Hoare logic that introduces “ghost variables” to extend the expressive power of pre-/postconditions. Ghost variables are variables that do not actually occur in the program and are allowed to have arbitrary quantum states (in a sense, they are existentially quantified), and be entangled with program variables. Ghost variables allow us to express properties such as the distribution of a program variable or the fact that a variable has classical content. And as a case study, we show how quantum Hoare logic with ghost variables can be used to prove the security of the quantum one-time pad.



Contents

1
Introduction

Designing algorithms is an inherently error-prone process. This is
especially true for quantum algorithms. Quantum algorithms can solve
certain computational problems much faster than classical computers
(e.g.,
[
18
,
10
,
11
]
), and most
likely will be of great impact once quantum computers are available.
And already now, analyzing quantum algorithms is of practical
relevance when proving the security of cryptosystems against future
quantum attackers (post-quantum cryptography). But quantum algorithms
are difficult to get right: Quantum mechanics has many properties that
go against human intuition, and quantum programs are difficult to test
and debug since we cannot directly observe their state (except in
small-scale simulations). A solution to this problem is formal
verification where we prove the behavior of the algorithm. In the
classical realm, Hoare logic
[
12
]
(and its close relative, the predicate
transformers
[
8
]
, which we treat as the same for the sake of
this introduction) has proven to be an invaluable tool for the
analysis of imperative programs. In Hoare logics, we analyze the
behavior of a program by investigating what “postcondition” the
final state of a program satisfies if the initial state satisfies a
certain “precondition”. This allows us to formally prove the
behavior of a complex program by first deriving the behavior of
individual lines of code and then modularly plugging these together to
get a description of the behavior of the whole program. Hoare logics
have been developed also for probabilistic programs

[
14
,
15
]
and quantum programs

[
7
,
20
,
5
,
9
,
13
]
.

However, existing Hoare logics still have limitations at to what can be
expressed within a pre-/postcondition. For example, we cannot express
that the value of a certain variable is uniformly distributed.
1
1

1

Classical/quantum calculi that use “expectations” (predicates that
do not just hold/not hold, but hold to a certain degree,

[
14
,
15
,
7
,
20
]
)
allow us to reason about probabilistic behavior. But they only
allow us to reason about the probability of a certain event (or the
expectation value of a quantity), but not about the distribution of
value. (I.e., we can express “
{\mathbf{x}}
has value
0
with probability
at least
\alpha
” but not “
{\mathbf{x}}
is uniform”.)
(E.g., to
state that inside a while-loop, we have the invariant that
{\mathbf{x}}

is a uniformly random bit.) Or, specific to the quantum case, that a
quantum variable has a certain distribution (e.g., is in the
“completely mixed state”, the quantum analogue to a uniform
distribution). Or
that a quantum variable is not entangled with other variables. Or that
a quantum variable contains classical data at a certain point in the
program. (Some logics distinguish
classical and quantum variables, e.g.,

[
19
]
,
at the costs of more complex semantics. But this does not allow us to
reason about dynamic properties, e.g., that a variable becomes
classical after a measurement.)

In this article, we present an extension of quantum Hoare logic that
removes these limitations. We introduce “ghost variables” and show
that using ghost variables, we can encode properties such as “
{\mathbf{x}}

has distribution
D
”
or “
{\mathbf{x}}

is separable (unentangled)” or “
{\mathbf{x}}

is classical”. (That is, all of these are emerging properties, not
hardcoded into our logic.) A ghost variable is a variable that does
not actually occur in the program but is introduced merely in a
predicate (pre-/postcondition). The ghost variable is then allowed to
take any value that makes the predicate true (effectively
existentially quantified). E.g., the classical predicate

{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{g}^{2}

(where
\mathbf{g}

is a ghost variable) would express that
{\mathbf{x}}

is a square. Classical ghost variables, however, do not yield any new
expressive power since existential quantifiers are already allowed in
most Hoare logics (so we could state the predicate as

\exists g.\,{\mathbf{x}}=g^{2}
).
In the quantum setting, however, a ghost variable can have a quantum
state, and possibly be entangled with other variables! For example,
if
\psi

is a maximally entangled state between two variables, then looking at
only one of those variables, we would see a uniformly distributed
variable. And a uniformly distributed variable can always be seen as
part of a system with two variables in state
\psi
.
Thus the predicate “
{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}

together are in state
\psi
”
(where
\mathbf{e}

is a ghost variable) models the fact that
{\mathbf{x}}

is uniformly distributed. Similarly we can encode classicality and
separability. Thus, using ghost variables, we can continue reasoning
about quantum programs using Hoare logic, but additionally have
program invariants that state that variables are distributed in
certain ways, are classical, are separable, and more. (We stress that
this even is an advance over the state of the art for classical
programs. Our logic could be used in the analysis of classical
programs to express the fact that certain variables have certain
distributions. This is quite unexpected since we would be using a
quantum phenomenon to analyze purely classical programs.)

Additionally the introduction of ghost variables makes the foundations
of the investigated programming language simpler. Many operations that
one thinks of as elementary (such as random sampling, measurements)
can actually be built from more elementary operations (such as
applying a unitary operation, initializing a quantum register). Using
ghost variables we can then derive the properties of the derived
operations from the properties of the elementary one. (E.g., show
that after random sampling the assigned variable has a certain
distribution and is classical.) This means that the language is
simpler (and thus arguably more foundationally elegant), and the core
set of rules of our logic is quite small (eleven rules).

Finally, we demonstrate that our logic can be applied to problems that
seem out of reach of existing Hoare logics: We analyze quantum
one-time pad encryption and show that it is secure, i.e., that an encrypted
quantum message indeed “looks random”.

Related work.

Hoare logic was first introduced by Hoare

[
12
]
. A different view was provided by Dijkstra

[
8
]
using predicate transformers. Hoare
logics/predicate transformers were generalized by Kozen

[
14
]
(and
[
15
]
for the case
of combined probabilism/nondeterminism).
Quantum Hoare logics and predicate transformer calculi for quantum programs
have been presented by D’Hondt and Panangaden
[
7
]
,
Chadha, Mateus and Sernadas
[
5
]
, Feng, Duan, Ji,
and Ying
[
9
]
, Ying
[
20
]
,
and Kakutani
[
13
]
. Unruh
[
19
]
gives a quantum
Hoare logic for analyzing pairs of programs (based on the classical
pRHL
[
2
]
). A different approach is taken by
pictorial calculi where quantum processes can be formalized and
rewritten as diagrams, starting with Abramsky and Coecke

[
1
]
.
[
6
, Example 4.91]

applies this approach to the classical one-time pad, but only to its
correctness, not its security. The quantum one-time pad was
discovered by
[
3
,
16
]
.

Organisation.

Section 2
introduces some quantum basics as well as
important notation and auxiliary concepts.
Section 3

introduces syntax and semantics of the simple imperative quantum
language we use for our calculus. (And explains how random sampling,
measurements, etc. are encoded using more basic language features.)

Section 4
introduces our Hoare logic with
ghosts. (The concept of ghost variables and the semantics of Hoare judgments.)

Section 5
shows how important properties such as
distributions of variables, classicality, separability can be encoded
in pre-/postconditions using ghost variables.

Section 6
presents and explains the eleven core rules
of the logic from which all other rules can be derived.

Section 7
derives a number of additional rules from
the core rules. (For reasoning about derived language features, and
for convenient reasoning about programs with classical variables.)

Section 8
analyses the quantum one-time pad.

2
Preliminaries: Variables, Memories, and Predicates

In this section, we introduce some fundamental concepts and notations
needed for this paper, and recap some of the needed quantum background
as we go along.
When introducing some notation
X
, the place of definition is marked like this:
X
. All symbols are listed in the symbol index.

Variables.

Before we introduce the syntax and semantics of programs, we first
need to introduce some basic concepts. A

variable
is described by a variable name
{\mathbf{x}}

that identifies the variable, and a type
T
. The
type
of
{\mathbf{x}}

is simply the set of all (classical) values the variable can
take. E.g., a variable might have type
\{0,1\}
,
or
\mathbb{N}
.
2
2

2

We
stress that we do not assume that the type is a finite or even a
countable set. Consequently, the Hilbert spaces considered in this
paper are not necessarily finite dimensional or even
separable. However, all results can be informally understood by
thinking of all sets as finite and hence of all Hilbert spaces as

\mathbb{C}^{N}

for suitable
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

We will assume that there is always some distinguished value in
T

that we denote
0
.

We distinguish between three kinds of variables: program variables

{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{z}}

(that can occur in programs), entangled ghost variables

\mathbf{e}
,
and unentangled ghost variables
\mathbf{u}
. We write

\mathbf{g}
for variables that are entangled ghosts or unentangled ghosts.
(The meaning of these kinds will become clear later, for now they
simply form a partition of the set of all variables.) We use

{\mathbf{v}},{\mathbf{w}}
when we do not wish to specify the kind of variable.

Lists or sets of variables will be denoted
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}
or

\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Z}
or

\mathbf{E}
or

\mathbf{U}
or

\mathbf{G}

(depending on the kind of variable they contain).
Given a list

\mathbf{V}={\mathbf{v}}_{1}\dots{\mathbf{v}}_{n}

of variables, we say its
type
is
T_{1}\times\dots\times T_{n}

if
T_{i}
is the type of
{\mathbf{v}}_{i}
.
We write
\mathsf{progvars}(\mathbf{V})
for the program
variables in
\mathbf{V}
.

Memories and quantum states.

An
assignment
assigns to each variable a
classical value. Formally, for a set
\mathbf{V}
,
the
assignments over
\mathbf{V}

are all functions
{\mathsf{m}}

with domain
\mathbf{V}

such that: for all
{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbf{V}

with type
T_{\mathbf{x}}
,

{\mathsf{m}}({\mathbf{x}})\in T_{\mathbf{x}}
.
That is, assignments can represent the content of classical memories.

To model quantum memories, we simply consider superpositions of
assignments: A
(pure) quantum memory
is a superposition of
assignments. Formally,
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
,
the set of all quantum memories over
\mathbf{V}
,
is the Hilbert space with basis
3
3

3

When we say “basis”, we
always mean orthonormal basis.

\{\lvert{{\mathsf{m}}}\rangle\}_{\mathsf{m}}

where
{\mathsf{m}}

ranges over all assignments over
\mathbf{V}
. Here
\lvert{{\mathsf{m}}}\rangle
simply denotes the
basis vector labeled
{\mathsf{m}}
, we often write
\lvert{{\mathsf{m}}}\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}
to stress
which space we are talking about.
Intuitively, a quantum memory
\psi

over
\mathbf{V}

with
\lVert{\psi}\rVert=1

represents a state a quantum computer with variables
\mathbf{V}

could be in. (We do not require
\lVert{\psi}\rVert=1

for a quantum memories unless this is explicitly mentioned.)

We also consider quantum states over
arbitrary sets
X
(as opposed to sets of assignments).
Namely,
\ell^{2}(X)

denotes the Hilbert space with orthonormal basis
\{\lvert{x}\rangle\}_{x\in X}
.
(In that notation,
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]

is simply
\ell^{2}(A)

where
A

is the set of all assignments on
\mathbf{V}
.)
Elements
\psi\in\ell^{2}(X)

with
\lVert{\psi}\rVert=1

represent quantum states.

We often treat elements of
\ell^{2}(T)
and
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
interchangeably if
T
is
the type of
\mathbf{V}
since there is a natural isorphism between those spaces.

The tensor product
\otimes
combines two quantum states

\psi\in\ell^{2}(X),\phi\in\ell^{2}(Y)
into a joint system

\psi\otimes\phi\in\ell^{2}(X\times Y)
. In the case of quantum
memories
\psi,\phi
over
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}
, respectively,

\psi\otimes\phi\in\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}}]
.
(And
\psi\otimes\phi=\phi\otimes\psi
since we are composing
“named” systems.)

For a vector (or operator)
a
, we write
a^{*}
for its adjoint. (In the finite dimensional case, the adjoint
is simply the conjugate transpose of a vector/matrix. The literature
also knows the notation
a^{\dagger}
.) The adjoint of

\lvert{x}\rangle
is written
\langle{x}\rvert
.
We abbreviate
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\mathsf{proj}({\psi})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\mathsf{proj}({\psi})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\mathsf{proj}({\psi})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{proj}({\psi})$}}}:=\psi\psi^{*}
.
This is the projector onto
\psi
when
\lVert{\psi}\rVert=1
.

Mixed quantum memories.

In many situations, we need to model probabilistic quantum states
(e.g., a quantum state that is
\lvert{0}\rangle

with probability
\frac{1}{2}

and
\lvert{1}\rangle

with probability
\frac{1}{2}
). This is modeled using
mixed
states
(a.k.a.
density operators
). Having state
\psi_{i}

with probability
p_{i}

is represented by the operator
\rho:=\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{i}})
.
4
4

4

Mathematically,
these are the set of all positive Hermitian trace-class operators on

\ell^{2}(X)
.
The requirement “trace-class” ensures that the trace exists and
can be ignored in the finite-dimensional case.
5
5

5

Sums without
index set are always assumed to have an arbitrary (not necessarily finite or even
countable) index set. In the case of sums of vectors in a Hilbert space,
convergence is with respect to the Hilbert space norm, and in the case of sums
of positive operators, the convergence is with respect to the Loewner order.

Then
\rho

encodes all observable information about the distribution of the
quantum state (that is, two distributions of quantum states have the
same
\rho

iff they cannot be distinguished by any physical process). And

\operatorname{tr}\rho

is the total probability
\sum_{i}p_{i}
.
(That is,
\operatorname{tr}\rho=1

unless we wish to represent the outcome of a non-terminating program.)
We will often need
to consider mixed states of quantum memories (i.e., mixed states with
underlying Hilbert space
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
). We call them

mixed (quantum) memories
over
\mathbf{V}
.

For a mixed memory
\rho

over
\mathbf{V}\supseteq\mathbf{W}
the
partial trace

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{W}}\rho

is the result of throwing away variables
\mathbf{W}

(i.e., it is a mixed memory over
\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W}
).
Formally,
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{W}}
is defined as the continuous linear function satisfying

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{W}}(\sigma\otimes\tau):=\sigma\cdot\operatorname{tr}\tau
where
\tau
is
an operator over
\mathbf{W}
.

A mixed memory
\rho

is
(\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W})
-separable

(i.e., not entangled between
\mathbf{V}
and
\mathbf{W}
) iff it can be written as

\rho=\sum_{i}\rho_{i}\otimes\rho_{i}^{\prime}
for mixed memories

\rho_{i},\rho_{i}^{\prime}
over
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}
, respectively.

Operations on quantum states.

An operation on a quantum state is modeled by an isometry
U

on
\ell^{2}(X)
.
6
6

6

That
is, a norm-preserving linear operation. Often, one models quantum
operations as unitaries instead because in the finite-dimensional
case an isometry is automatically unitary. However, in the
infinite-dimensional case, unitaries are unnecessarily
restrictive. Consider, e.g., the isometry
\lvert{i}\rangle\mapsto\lvert{i+1}\rangle

with
i\in\mathbb{N}

which is a perfectly valid quantum operation but not a unitary.
If
we apply such an operation on a mixed state
\rho
,
the result is
U\rho U^{*}
.

Most often,
isometries will occur in the context of operations that are performed
on a single variable or list of variables, i.e., an isometry
U

on
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
.
Then
U

can also be applied to
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{W}}]

with
\mathbf{W}\supseteq\mathbf{V}
:
we identify
U

with
U\otimes\mathsf{id}_{\mathbf{W}\setminus\mathbf{V}}
.
Furthermore, if
\mathbf{V}

has type
T
,
then an isometry
U

on
\ell^{2}(T)

can be seen as an isometry on
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]

since we identify
\ell^{2}(T)
and
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
. If we want to make

\mathbf{W}
explicit, we write
U\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{W}
for the isometry
U
on
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
.
For example, if
U
is a
2\times 2
-matrix and
{\mathbf{x}}
has type bit,
then
U\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{x}}
can be applied to quantum memories over
{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}
,
acting on
{\mathbf{x}}
only.
This notation is not limited to isometries, of course, but applies
to other operators, too.
(By “operator” we always mean a bounded linear operator in this paper.)

An important operation is
\mathsf{CNOT}

on
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}

(where
\mathbf{V},\mathbf{W}

both have type
\{0,1\}^{n}
),
defined by

\mathsf{CNOT}(\lvert{x}\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes\lvert{y}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}}):=\lvert{x}\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes\lvert{x\oplus y}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}}
. (That is, we allow
\mathsf{CNOT}

not only on single bits but bitstrings.)

Predicates.

In Hoare judgments, we need to express
properties of the state of a quantum memory. In this paper, we only
consider properties that are closed under superpositions of quantum
states. That is, a
predicate

\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C}
on
\mathbf{V}

is a subspace
7
7

7

By subspace, we always mean closed
subspaces. In the finite-dimensional case, all subspaces are closed
anyway.
of
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
.
The syntax of predicates will not be fixed to a specific language,
i.e., any mathematically expressible subspace is a valid
predicate. But we fix some syntactic sugar for expressing
predicates succinctly:

•

\top
,

\bot
:
The predicate that is always satisfied is denoted

\top:=\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
. The predicate that is never satisfied is
\bot:=\{0\}
.

•

\mathsf{A}\wedge\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}
,
\mathsf{A}\vee\mathsf{B}
:
To model that both
\mathsf{A}

and
\mathsf{B}

hold (conjunction), we simply use the intersection of
\mathsf{A}

and
\mathsf{B}

(as sets). That is, we write
\mathsf{A}\land\mathsf{B}

to denote
\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{B}
.
We will often also write this as “
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}
”
instead of “
\mathsf{A}\land\mathsf{B}
”
where “
,
”
is understood to bind less closely than “
\vee
”.
To model that
\mathsf{A}

or
\mathsf{B}

holds (disjunction), we use the sum
\mathsf{A}+\mathsf{B}

(the space of all linear combinations from
\mathsf{A}

and
\mathsf{B}
).
That is, we write
\mathsf{A}\vee\mathsf{B}

to denote
\mathsf{A}+\mathsf{B}
.
This choice of connectives corresponds to Birkhoff-von Neumann
quantum logic. But we stress that we are not restricted to using
only these connectives, we may use any well-defined operations on
subspaces. These are just the ones that will turn out useful in the
remainder of the paper.

•

M\cdot\mathsf{A}
:
For an operator
M

(typically an isometry or a projector) and a predicate
\mathsf{A}
,
we write
M\cdot\mathsf{A}

for the subspace
\{M\psi:\psi\in\mathsf{A}\}
.
Thus,
M\cdot\mathsf{A}

is satisfied if we apply
M
to a quantum memory in
\mathsf{A}
.

•

\mathbf{X}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S
:
For some variable list
\mathbf{X}

of type
T
,
we may wish to express the fact that the value of
\mathbf{X}

lies in a certain subspace
S\subseteq\ell^{2}(T)
.
Notice that
S

can be naturally seen as a subspace of
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
.
Then
\mathbf{V}

being in a state in
S

means that the state of the whole quantum memory is in

S\otimes\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}}]
.
We introduce the syntactic sugar “
\mathbf{X}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S
”
to denote
S\otimes\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{X}}]
.
Note that even though it looks like a Boolean expression, it actually is a subspace
of
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}}]
and thus a predicate in our sense.

•

\mathbf{W}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi
.
Often, we will also want to express that the variables
\mathbf{W}

are in a specific state
\psi
.
This means the variables lie in
\operatorname{span}\{\psi\}
,
using the previous syntactic sugar we can write this as

\mathbf{W}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}\operatorname{span}\{\psi\}
.
We introduce the abbreviation
\mathbf{W}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi

for this common case.

•

\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{W}/\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\}
.
Renaming variables
\mathbf{W}

to
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

in predicate
\mathsf{A}
.
This assumes
\mathsf{A}

is a predicate over
\mathbf{V}\supseteq\mathbf{W}
,
that
\mathbf{W}\cap(\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W})=\varnothing
,
and that
\mathbf{W}

and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

have the same type. Then
\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{W}/\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\}

is the predicate over
({\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W}})\mathbin{\mathaccent 0{\cdot}\cup}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

defined by

U_{\mathbf{W}\to\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathsf{A}

where
U_{\mathbf{W}\to\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

is the natural isomorphism between
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{W}}]

and
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}]
,
i.e.,
U_{\mathbf{W}\to\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}}:=\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

for all
i
.
(
{U_{\mathbf{W}\to\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}}

renames
\mathbf{W}
to
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
when applied to a quantum memory.)

An example
\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{example}}
of a predicate would be:

\bigl({{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{00}\rangle+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{11}\rangle}\bigr)\vee\bigl({{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr),\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{1}\rangle

This means, intuitively, that
{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}

are maximally entangled (in state
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{00}\rangle+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{11}\rangle

up to a global phase factor) or
{\mathbf{x}}

has state
\lvert{0}\rangle
, and in addition
{\mathbf{z}}
has state
\lvert{1}\rangle
.

Note that our predicates seems to be lacking in expressiveness
compared with the predicates, e.g., from
[
19
]
: It is not
possible to parameterize the predicate using the values of classical
variables. E.g., we cannot write
{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{{\mathbf{x}}}\rangle

where
{\mathbf{x}}

is a classical variable. This is because our semantics does not
hardcode the distinction between classical and quantum variables (all
variables are quantum by default). But, in
Section 7.2
, we will see
how to express classical variables as a derived feature, and introduce
additional syntactic sugar that allows us to recover the full
expressiveness of the predicates from
[
19
]
.

Given a predicate
\mathsf{A}
,
we will often wish to indicate which variables it talks about, i.e.,
what are its
free variables
. Since our definition of predicates is semantic
(i.e., we are not limited to predicates expressed using the syntax
above) we cannot simply speak about the variables occurring in the
expression describing
\mathsf{A}
.
Instead, we say
\mathsf{A}

contains only variables from
\mathbf{W}

(written:
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})$}}}\subseteq\mathbf{W}
)
iff there exists a subspace
S

such that
\mathsf{A}=(\mathbf{W}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S)
.
(That is, if
\mathsf{A}

can be described solely in terms of the content of the variables

\mathbf{W}
.)
Note that there is a certain abuse of notation here: We formally
defined “
\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})\subseteq\mathbf{W}
”,
but we do not define
\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})
;

\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})\subseteq\mathbf{W}

should formally just be seen as an abbreviation for

\exists S.\,\mathsf{A}=(\mathbf{W}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S)
.
8
8

8

In
fact, defining
\mathsf{fv}(A)

is possible only if there is a smallest set
\mathbf{W}

such that
\exists S.\ \mathsf{A}=(\mathbf{W}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S)
.
This is not necessarily the case. For example, assume that
\mathbf{V}

is infinite, let
\mathsf{A}

be the space spanned by all
\lvert{{\mathsf{m}}}\rangle

where
{\mathsf{m}}({\mathbf{v}})\neq 0

for only finitely many
{\mathbf{v}}\in\mathbf{V}
. Then
\mathsf{A}=(\mathbf{W}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S)

for any cofinite
\mathbf{W}

(by defining
S

as the span of all
\lvert{{\mathsf{m}}}\rangle

with only finitely many
{\mathsf{m}}({\mathbf{v}})\neq 0
),
but
\mathsf{A}\neq(\mathbf{W}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S)

whenever
\mathbf{W}

is not cofinite. Since there is no smallest cofinite
\mathbf{W}
,

\mathsf{fv}(A)

cannot be defined, but we can still meaningfully use the notation

\mathsf{fv}(A)\subseteq\mathbf{W}
.

For example,
\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A}_{\mathrm{example}})\subseteq\{{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}},{\mathbf{z}}\}
.
Similarly, we treat
\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})\cap\mathbf{W}=\varnothing
.

If two predicates
\mathsf{A}

and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}

on variables
\mathbf{V}

and
\mathbf{W}
,
respectively, satisfy

\mathsf{A}\otimes\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{W}\setminus\mathbf{V}}]=\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\otimes\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W}}]
,
then
\mathsf{A}

and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}

intuitively describe the same property on the shared variables

\mathbf{V}\cap\mathbf{W}

(and say nothing about the remaining variables). Therefore we will

identify

such
\mathsf{A}

and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}

throughout this paper. In particular, any predicate
\mathsf{A}

can be seen as a predicate
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})
.

3
Quantum programs

Syntax.

We will now define a small imperative quantum language. The set of all
programs is described by the following syntax:

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}$}}}::={\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}\ |\ \underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\ |\ \underline{\mathbf{if}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \underline{\mathbf{then}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\ \underline{\mathbf{else}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\ |\ \underline{\mathbf{while}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \underline{\mathbf{do}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\ |\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}};\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\ |\ \underline{\mathbf{skip}}

Here
\mathbf{X}

is a list of program variables,
{\mathbf{x}}

a program variable,
{\mathbf{y}}

a program variable of type
\{0,1\}
,
and
U

an isometry on
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{X}}]

(there is no fixed set of allowed isometries, any isometry that we can
describe can be used here).
9
9

9

We will assume throughout the
paper that all programs satisfy those well-typedness constraints. In
particular, rules may implicitly impose type constraints on the
variables and constants occurring in them by this assumption.

Intuitively,
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}

means that the operation
U

is applied to the quantum variables
\mathbf{X}
.
E.g.,
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ H\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}

would apply the Hadamard gate to the variable
{\mathbf{x}}

(we assume that
H

denote the Hadamard matrix). It is important that we can apply
U

to several variables
\mathbf{X}
simultaneously,
otherwise no entanglement between variables can ever be produced.

The program
\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}

initializes
{\mathbf{x}}

with the quantum state
\lvert{0}\rangle
.
(Remember that we assumed that every variable type contains a
distinguished element
0
.)

The program
\underline{\mathbf{if}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \underline{\mathbf{then}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\ \underline{\mathbf{else}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}

will measure the qubit
{\mathbf{y}}
,
and, if the outcome is
1
, execute
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
, otherwise execute
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}
.

The program
\underline{\mathbf{while}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \underline{\mathbf{do}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

measures
{\mathbf{y}}
,
and if the outcome is
1
,
it executes
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
. This is repeated until the outcome is
0
.

Finally,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}};\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}

executes
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

and then
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}
.
And
\underline{\mathbf{skip}}

does nothing. We will always implicitly treat “
;
”
as associative and
\underline{\mathbf{skip}}
as its neutral element.

On the minimalism of the language.

This language is intentionally minimalistic. It seems to lack a number
of features that are present, e.g., in
[
19
]
: Initializing
variables with states other than
\lvert{0}\rangle
.
Performing measurements. Probabilism (i.e., random
sampling). Parameterizing operations/states using classical variables
(e.g.,
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ R_{\mathbf{x}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}

where
{\mathbf{x}}

is a variable of type
\mathbb{R}
,
and
R_{\theta}

a rotation by angle
\theta
).
All these features are very important if we want to model anything but
the simplest programs. Yet, as we will see, these features are not
actually lacking. Using syntactic sugar (introduced in this section
and in
Section 7.2
), we can recover all those
features. Keeping the language minimal and encoding all advanced
features allows us to get a much simpler core logic. Rules for working
with the advanced features can then be derived from the core features.

Semantics.

The
denotational semantics
of our programs
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

are represented as functions

\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket
on the mixed memories over

\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}
, defined
by recursion on the structure of the programs. Here
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}

is a fixed set of program variables, and we will assume that

\mathsf{fv}(\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}})\subseteq\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}

for all programs in this paper.
10
10

10

We fix some set
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}

in order to avoid a more cumbersome notation
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{X}}

where we explicitly indicate the set
\mathbf{X}
of program
variables with respect to which the semantics is defined.

The obvious cases are
\llbracket{\underline{\mathbf{skip}}}\rrbracket:=\mathsf{id}

and
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}};\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket:=\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket\circ\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket
.
And application of an isometry
U

is also fairly straightforward given the syntactic sugar introduced
above:

\llbracket{{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}}\rrbracket(\rho):=({U\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\rho({U\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})^{*}
.

Initialization of a quantum variable is slightly more complicated:

\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}

initializes the variable
{\mathbf{x}}

with
\lvert{0}\rangle
,
which is the same as removing
{\mathbf{x}}
,
and then creating a new variable
{\mathbf{x}}

with content
\lvert{0}\rangle
.
Removing
{\mathbf{x}}

is done by the operation
\operatorname{tr}_{{\mathbf{x}}}

(partial trace, see
\autopageref
page:partr). And creating a new variable
{\mathbf{x}}

is done by the operation
\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}})
.
Thus we define
\llbracket{\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}}\rrbracket(\rho):=\operatorname{tr}_{{\mathbf{x}}}\rho\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}})
.

The if-command first performs a measurement and then branches. A
measurement is described by one projector for each outcome. In our
case,
\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}

corresponds to outcome
i=0,1
.
We then have that the state after measurement (without
renormalization) is

({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})\rho({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})^{*}
. Then
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

or
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}

is applied to that state and the resulting states are added together
to get the final mixed state. Altogether:

\llbracket{\underline{\mathbf{if}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \underline{\mathbf{then}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\ \underline{\mathbf{else}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket(\rho):=\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket\bigl({\mathord{\downarrow_{1}}(\rho)}\bigr)+\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket\bigl({\mathord{\downarrow_{0}}(\rho)}\bigr)\\
\text{where}\qquad{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\mathord{\downarrow_{i}}(\rho)$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\mathord{\downarrow_{i}}(\rho)$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\mathord{\downarrow_{i}}(\rho)$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathord{\downarrow_{i}}(\rho)$}}}:=({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})\rho({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})^{*}

While-commands are modeled similar: In an execution of a while
statement, we have
n\geq 0

iterations of “measure with outcome
1

and run
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
”
(which applies
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket\circ\mathord{\downarrow_{1}}

to the state), followed by “measure with outcome
0
”
(which applies
\mathord{\downarrow_{0}}

to the state). Adding all those branches up, we get the
definition:

\llbracket{\underline{\mathbf{while}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \underline{\mathbf{do}}\ \boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket(\rho):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathord{\downarrow_{0}}\bigl({(\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket\circ\mathord{\downarrow_{1}})^{n}(\rho)}\bigr)

Syntactic sugar.

To work productively with the minimal
language from above, we introduce some syntactic sugar:

•

\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi
(initialization with quantum state): To assign a quantum state

\psi\in\ell^{2}(T)

to variables
\mathbf{X}
of type
T
,
we have to do the following: We fix an isometry
U_{\psi}

with
U\lvert{0,\dots,0}\rangle=\psi
.
And then we initialize all
\mathbf{X}

with
\lvert{0}\rangle

and apply
U
.
That is,
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi

abbreviates “
\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}_{1};\dots;\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}_{n};{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U_{\psi}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}
”
with
{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\dots{\mathbf{x}}_{n}:=\mathbf{X}

and some arbitrary isometry
U_{\psi}\lvert{0,\dots,0}\rangle:=\psi
.
(Such
U_{\psi}
is not unique but always exists.)

•

\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z
(classical initialization / assign-statement): This is short for

\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{z}\rangle
. (We assume that
z
is in the type of
\mathbf{X}
.)

•

\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
(measurement). We wish to simulate a measurement in the
computational basis using the commands from our minimal language.
It is a well-known (and easy to check) fact that measuring
\mathbf{X}

and assigning
\lvert{z}\rangle

to
\mathbf{Y}

(where
z

is the outcome) is equivalent to performing a CNOT from
\mathbf{X}
to a

\lvert{0}\rangle
-initialized
\mathbf{Y}
and to a
\lvert{0}\rangle
-initialized auxiliary
register and discarding the auxiliary register. This can be expressed
using our language: We define
\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}

to denote
“
\mathbf{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle;{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle;{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{Y};{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle
”
where
{\mathbf{z}}

is a fresh variable of the same type as
\mathbf{Y}

and
\mathbf{X}
.
(I.e.,
{\mathbf{z}}
is a variable that is used nowhere else.)

Similarly, we can also define a measurement of
\mathbf{X}

that does not remember the outcome. (That is, its effect is merely
to change the measured variables.) We write
\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}

to denote
“
{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle;{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle
”
where
{\mathbf{z}}

is a fresh variable of the same type as
\mathbf{X}
.

(Of course, it is also possible to model measurements other than
computational basis measurements. To implement a projective
measurement described by projectors
\{P_{i}\}_{i}
,
we simply replace
\mathsf{CNOT}

by the unitary
\sum_{i}P_{i}\otimes U_{\oplus i}

where
U_{\oplus i}:\lvert{z}\rangle\mapsto\lvert{z\oplus i}\rangle
.
We do not fix a specific syntax for this construction.)

•

\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D
(random sampling). Here
D

is a discrete probability distribution over
T
,
the type of
\mathbf{X}
.
Sampling for
D

is easily done by initializing
\mathbf{X}

in the state
{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\psi_{D}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\psi_{D}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\psi_{D}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\psi_{D}$}}}:=\sum_{i\in T}\sqrt{D(i)}\lvert{i}\rangle

and then measuring that state in the computational basis (leaving

\mathbf{X}

in state
\lvert{i}\rangle

with probability
D(i)
).
That is,
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D

is shorthand for “
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi_{D};\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
.”

4
Hoare Logic with Ghosts

Recap Hoare logic.

Before we introduce our Hoare logic with ghost variables, we quickly
recap regular quantum Hoare logic.
11
11

11

Strictly speaking,
“recap” is not the right word since as far as we know this variant
of quantum Hoare logic has not explicitly been spelled out in the
literature. However, we still consider it folklore because it is a
relatively simple generalization of
[
4
]
(allowing for more
general programs, density operator based semantics, and changing the
presentation from weakest precondition transformers to Hoare
triples), it is a simplification of
[
19
]
(which
considers pairs of programs instead of single programs), and it is a
special case of
[
20
]
(by considering only “strict”
predicates there, i.e., predicates that are projectors, we get a
logic that is roughly the same).
Intuitively, a Hoare triple

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

means: If the initial state
\rho

of the program
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

satisfies
\mathsf{A}
,
and we run the program
\mathsf{B}
,
then the final state
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket(\rho)

satisfies
\mathsf{B}
.
Since the states of programs in our semantics are mixed
memories
\rho

(i.e., density operators), we need to first define what it means for a
mixed memory to satisfy a predicate. For this, the notion of
support of a density operator comes in handy: A density operator can
always be represented as
\rho=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{i}})
,
and intuitively this means that
\rho

is a mixture of states
\psi_{i}
.
(But note that this decomposition is not unique!) Then

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho$}}}:=\operatorname{span}\{\psi_{i}\}_{i}

is simply the subspace spanned by all the vectors that constitute

\rho
.
12
12

12

The
usual formal definition of
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho

is
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho:=\operatorname{im}P

where
P

is the smallest projector such that
P\rho P^{*}=\rho
.
(This definition has the advantage of not requiring a specific
choice of decomposition of
\rho
.)
But it is easy to verify that this definition coincides with

\operatorname{span}\{\psi_{i}\}_{i}
.

(Fortunately, this definition turns out to be independent of the
choice of
\psi_{i}
.)
Now, if
\mathsf{A}

is a predicate over the program variables
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}

(formally: a subspace of
\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}}]
),
and
\rho

is a mixed memory over
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}
,
then
\rho

satisfies
\mathsf{A}

(written
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}
)
iff
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho\subseteq\mathsf{A}
.
(I.e., iff
\rho

is a mixture of quantum memories in
A
.)
With this notation (that will be changed somewhat later to accommodate
ghost variables), we can formally define
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

as: for all
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}

we have
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket(\rho)\vDash\mathsf{B}
. (We call
\mathsf{A}
the
precondition

and
\mathsf{B}
the
postcondition
.)
For this logic, we can then prove a number of rules that allow us to
derive the behavior of a complex quantum program from the behavior of
its elementary building blocks. For example, the (very easy to
prove)
Seq
rule shows that
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

and
\{{\mathsf{B}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\,}\{{\mathsf{C}}\}

implies
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}};\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\,}\{{\mathsf{C}}\}
.
This allows us to break down the analysis of a sequence of commands
into an analysis of the individual commands. (And similar rules exist
for quantum operations, while-loops, etc.)

However, this Hoare logic is somewhat limited in its expressivity. For
example, we cannot express the fact that the variable
{\mathbf{x}}

is uniformly randomly distributed. Say
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

samples
{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D

where
D

is the uniform distribution. Then the final state is

\rho=\sum_{i\in T}\frac{1}{\lvert{T}\rvert}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})

where
T

is the type of
{\mathbf{x}}
,
and
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho=\operatorname{span}\{\lvert{i}\rangle\}_{i\in T}=\ell^{2}[{{\mathbf{x}}}]=\top
.
So the only postcondition for this
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

is
\top
,
the trivial postcondition. So the above quantum Hoare logic forgets
about the distribution of
{\mathbf{x}}

and remembers only what values have non-zero probability. (I.e.,
probabilism is treated as possibilistic nondeterminism.) For similar
reasons, we cannot express, say, that
{\mathbf{x}}

is classical (e.g., after the program
\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ {\mathbf{x}}
).

Extensions of this basic quantum Hoare logic can make some statements
about probabilities: Quantum Hoare logic whose pre-/postconditions are
expectations
[
7
,
20
]
can, e.g., express
that a certain predicate will hold with a certain probability. And

[
19
]
can express that the outputs of two programs are
identical, even taking into account their distributions. But both
still lack the possibility of stating, as part of a
pre-/postcondition, e.g., that a variable has a particular
distribution.
See also discussion in
Section 8.2
for
further discussion on the limitations of those logics.

Ghost variables.

Our solution to this problem is the introduction of “ghost
variables” (which will will often simply call “ghosts” for
brevity). In our context, a ghost variable is a variable that cannot
occur in the program (nor in the memory of the program) but only in
predicates. The intuitive meaning of a ghost variable is that it can
take any value that makes a predicate true. To illustrate the idea,
let us forget about quantum programs for a moment and consider the
classical case: For example, the classical postcondition
{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{g}^{2}

would mean that after the execution of
the program, the variable
{\mathbf{x}}

contains the square of
\mathbf{g}
,
if
{\mathbf{x}}

and
\mathbf{g}

are both program variables of type
\mathbb{N}
. But if
\mathbf{g}

is a ghost, then
{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{g}^{2}

is true whenever there is some way to assign an integer to
\mathbf{g}

that makes
{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{g}^{2}

true. In other words, the postcondition
{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{g}^{2}

is equivalent to just saying that
{\mathbf{x}}

is a square. Now, in the classical case this is not very impressive:

{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{g}^{2}

is just equivalent to
\exists z.\,{\mathbf{x}}=z^{2}
.
And any other predicate involving ghosts can also be
rewritten into a regular predicate by using existential
quantifiers. So, at least if we allow existential quantifiers in
predicates (and there is no reason why we should not),
ghost
variables are useless for classical Hoare logic
.
13
13

13

Which is,
most likely, why they have not been considered before.
However,
this argument does not apply in the quantum case. A quantum ghost
variable cannot just be simulated using an existential quantifier
(e.g., because ghost variables might be entangled with quantum
variables).

So, how can we formalize ghost variables in the quantum setting? A
classical memory
{\mathsf{m}}

(containing only program variables) satisfies a predicate
\mathsf{A}

involving ghosts iff there exists a larger memory

{\mathsf{m}}^{\circ}
containing both program and ghost variables such that

{\mathsf{m}}^{\circ}

satisfies
\mathsf{A}
,
and
{\mathsf{m}}

is the result of removing all ghosts from
{\mathsf{m}}^{\circ}
.
The quantum analogue of removing variables is the partial trace. That
is, if we have a mixed memory
\rho

on
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{G}
,
then
\rho_{\mathbf{X}}:=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{G}}\rho

is the result of removing all ghosts
\mathbf{G}

from
\rho
.
Thus, we are ready for our first tentative definition:
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}

iff there exists a density operator
\rho^{\circ}
on
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{G}
such that
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{G}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho
and

\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathsf{A}
.

Note that in the previous definition, the program variables
\mathbf{X}

and the ghost variables
\mathbf{G}

can be entangled in arbitrary ways (since we put no restriction on

\rho^{\circ}
).
However, there is a different possibility of defining ghost variables:
We could additionally require that
\rho^{\circ}

is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{G})
-separable.
That would mean that ghost and program variables may not be
entangled. This will lead to a very different behavior of ghost
variables. It will turn out that both variants have their uses, so in
our logic we will simply consider both variants: We consider two kinds
of ghost variables, entangled ghost variables
\mathbf{E}

and unentangled ghost variables
\mathbf{U}
.
That is,
\mathsf{A}

may contain both entangled and unentangled ghosts, and

\rho^{\circ}

is required to be
(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U})
-separable.
This means that the variables
\mathbf{U}

cannot be entangled with the program variables
\mathbf{X}
,
but the variable
\mathbf{E}
can be!

Formal definitions.

We can now mold all these ideas into a formal definition:

Definition 1
(Satisfying a predicate with ghosts)

Let
\rho

be a mixed memory over
\mathbf{X}
.
Let
\mathsf{A}

be a predicate over
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}
.
Then a density operator
\rho
over
\mathbf{X}

satisfies

\mathsf{A}

(written
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}
)
iff there exists a
(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U})
-separable mixed memory
\rho^{\circ}
over
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}
such that

\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathsf{A}
and
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho
.

Recall that we use different letters for different kinds of
variables (cf.
\autopageref
page:variable.conventions),
so the above definition implicitly assumes that

\mathbf{X}

are program variables,
\mathbf{E}

are entangled ghost variables, and
\mathbf{U}

are unentangled ghost variables. In the remainder of this work, we
assume that these conventions are understood.
Note that if
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{U}=\varnothing
,
then
Definition 1
specializes to the definition given in the recap above,
namely
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}\iff\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho\subseteq\mathsf{A}
.

Given the definition of satisfying a predicate, it is straightforward
to define our Hoare logic:

Definition 2
(Hoare logic with ghosts)

Let
\mathsf{A}

be a predicate over
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}
,
and
\mathsf{B}

a predicate over
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\mathbf{U}^{\prime}
,
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
a program.

Then
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

iff for all mixed memories
\rho

over
\mathbf{X}^{\mathbf{all}}

with
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}
, we have that
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket(\rho)\vDash\mathsf{B}
.

Note that
\mathsf{A}

and
\mathsf{B}

do not need to use the same ghosts. Ghosts are local
to the interpretation of a given predicate. In particular, if ghost
variables are chosen in a particular way when showing
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}
,
this does not mean that they have to be chosen in a related way in

\rho\vDash\mathsf{B}
!

Example.

Consider the following situation. We have two
variables
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}

of type
\{0,1\}
.
Initially, they are entangled in the state

\psi:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{00}\rangle+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{11}\rangle
.
Now we initialize
{\mathbf{y}}

with
\lvert{0}\rangle
.
What do we know about
{\mathbf{x}}
?
The initial state of
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{y}}

is represented by the predicate
{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi

in our notation (see
\autopageref
page:quanteq). So, we are asking for a predicate

\mathsf{B}

involving
{\mathbf{x}}

such that
\{{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

holds. Since
{\mathbf{x}}

is initially entangled with
{\mathbf{y}}
,
and
{\mathbf{y}}

is “overwritten” (thus effectively deleted),
{\mathbf{x}}

is afterwards entangled with a ghost (in a sense, the ghost
of the deleted
{\mathbf{y}}
).
That is,
\mathsf{B}=({\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi)
.
(The
Init
rule below will allow us to make this reasoning
rigorous.) And, as we will see later,
{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi

means that
{\mathbf{x}}

is a uniformly random bit. So we have derived that after deleting half
of an EPR pair
\psi
,
we get a uniformly random bit. (This matches what we know about EPR
pairs.)

About the sets of ghost variables.

The careful reader may
have noticed that there is an ambiguity in our notation. On

\autopageref
page:pred.identify, we said that we identify predicates
over different sets of variables as long as they coincide on their
free variables. But that means that for a state
\rho

over
\mathbf{X}
,
when interpreting
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}

according to
Definition 1
, we do not know what the sets
\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U}

are in that definition (we only know that

\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}\supseteq\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})\setminus\mathbf{X}
).
Fortunately, the following lemma shows that the choice of
\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}

is irrelevant, so our notational choice is justified. (Note that there
is no ambiguity concerning the set
\mathbf{X}

of program variables since that set is determined by the type of the
mixed memory
\rho
.)

Lemma 1
(Irrelevance of sets of ghosts)

Let
\rho

be a mixed memory over
\mathbf{X}
.
Let
\rho\vDash^{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\mathsf{A}

denote
\rho\vDash\mathsf{A}

(as in
Definition 1
) where
\mathsf{A}

is interpreted as a predicate over
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}
.

Assume that
\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1},\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}\supseteq\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})\setminus\mathbf{X}
.
Then
\rho\vDash^{\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}\mathsf{A}
iff
\rho\vDash^{\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}}\mathsf{A}
.

Proof.
For clarity, we write
\mathsf{A}_{\mathbf{V}}
when we interpret
\mathsf{A}
as a predicate over
\mathbf{V}
.

Due to symmetry, we only need to prove

\rho\vDash^{\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}\mathsf{A}\implies\rho\vDash^{\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}}\mathsf{A}
.
Assume
\rho\vDash^{\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}\mathsf{A}
.
Then there exists a
(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}_{1},\mathbf{U}_{1})
-separable
mixed memory
\rho^{\circ}

over
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}

with
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathsf{A}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}

and
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho
.

Let

\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}:=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}}\rho^{\circ}
.
Then
\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}

is a mixed memory over

\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2})(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})
.
And since
\rho^{\circ}

was
(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}_{1},\mathbf{U}_{1})
-separable,

\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}

is
(\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}),\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})
-separable.
And since
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathsf{A}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}
,
we have

\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}\subseteq\mathsf{A}_{\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2})(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})}
. (The last step uses that

\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2})(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})\supseteq\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})
.)

Let

\hat{\rho}^{\circ}:=\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{E}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{1}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{U}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{U}_{1}}

where
\sigma_{\mathbf{E}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{1}}

and
\sigma_{\mathbf{U}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{U}_{1}}

are arbitrary mixed memories of trace
1

over
\mathbf{E}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{1}

and
\mathbf{U}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{U}_{1}
,
respectively. Then
\hat{\rho}^{\circ}

is is

(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}_{2},\mathbf{U}_{2})=(\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2})(\mathbf{E}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{1}),(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})(\mathbf{U}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{U}_{1}))
-separable
since
\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}

is
(\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2}),\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})
-separable.
And

\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\hat{\rho}^{\circ}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}\otimes\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}]\subseteq\mathsf{A}_{\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2})(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})}\otimes\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}]=\mathsf{A}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}}
.
Furthermore

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}}\hat{\rho}^{\circ}=\operatorname{tr}_{(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2})(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})}\tilde{\rho}^{\circ}=\operatorname{tr}_{(\mathbf{E}_{1}\cap\mathbf{E}_{2})(\mathbf{U}_{1}\cap\mathbf{U}_{2})}\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}\setminus\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}}\rho^{\circ}=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{U}_{1}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho.

Thus
\rho\vDash^{\mathbf{E}_{2}\mathbf{U}_{2}}\mathsf{A}
.

\Box

5
Predicates with Ghosts

In this section, we describe three important kinds of predicates that
can be expressed using ghosts.

5.1
Variables with a certain distribution

First, we show that entangled ghosts can be used to express
that a variable has a certain distribution. Given a distribution
D

on
T
,
we define

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\psi_{DD}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\psi_{DD}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\psi_{DD}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\psi_{DD}$}}}:=\sum_{i}\sqrt{D(i)}\,\lvert{i}\rangle\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle
, a state on two variables of type
T
.
This state has the property that, if we erase (or measure) the second
part, we get a
D
-distributed
classical value
\lvert{i}\rangle

in the first part. So, if
{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}

is in state
\psi_{DD}
,
then, since
\mathbf{e}

is a ghost,
\mathbf{e}

is, in effect, erased. Thus the predicate
{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}=\psi_{DD}

effectively means that
{\mathbf{x}}

is
D
-distributed. Thus we introduce syntactic sugar for predicates:

•

\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
(
\mathbf{X}

is
D
-distributed).

\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})

is short for
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{DD}

where
\mathbf{e}

is a fresh entangled ghost (i.e., one that does not occur
elsewhere in the predicate we are formulating) of the same type as
\mathbf{X}
.

•

\mathbf{uniform}({\mathbf{X}})
(
\mathbf{X}

is uniformly distributed). This is short for
\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})

where
D
is the uniform distribution on the type of
\mathbf{X}
.

(As a special case, if
D

is the uniform distribution on a single bit, then
\psi_{DD}

is the state
\psi

from the example in the previous section. So the
postcondition
\mathsf{B}

in that example can indeed be written as
\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{x}}})

as was already hinted there.)

So far, we gave only a relatively hand-waving explanation why

\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})

means that
\mathbf{X}

is
D
-distributed. But the following lemma makes this formal:

Lemma 2
(Distribution predicates)

Let
D

be a distribution over
T
.
Let
\rho

be a mixed memory over
\mathbf{Y}
.
Let
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{Y}

have type
T
.
Let
\rho_{D}:=\sum_{i}D(i)\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})

be a mixed memory over
\mathbf{X}
.
(I.e.,
\rho_{D}

contains a
D
-distributed
classical value
i
.) Then the following are equivalent:

•

\rho\vDash\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
.

•

There exists a mixed memory
\rho^{\prime}

over
\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X}
such that
\rho=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\rho_{D}
.

In other words,
\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})

means that
\mathbf{X}

is
D
-distributed
and independent of other variables
.

Proof.
First, we check that
\rho_{D}

is the result of removing variable
\mathbf{e}

from the quantum memory
\psi_{DD}

(we interpret
\psi_{DD}
as a quantum memory over
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}
):

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{DD}})=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}}\mathsf{proj}\Bigl({\sum\nolimits_{i}\sqrt{D(i)}\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}}}\Bigr)=\sum\nolimits_{i}D(i)\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}})=\rho_{D}.

(1)

\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})

is syntactic sugar for
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{DD}

and thus is a predicate over some variables
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}

with
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{e}\in\mathbf{E}
.

“
\boldsymbol{\Longrightarrow}
”:

First we show that if
\rho\vDash\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
,
then
\rho=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\rho_{D}

for some
\rho^{\prime}
.
Since
\rho\vDash\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
,
there exists a
\rho^{\circ}

over
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}

with
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
and
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho
. Thus
\rho^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{i}})
for some
\psi_{i}
with

\psi_{i}\in\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})=(\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{DD})=\operatorname{span}\{{\psi_{DD}}\}\otimes\ell^{2}[{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}^{\complement}}].

Hence
\psi_{i}=\psi_{DD}\otimes\psi_{i}^{\prime}
for some
\psi_{i}^{\prime}
over
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}
. Thus

\rho=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{DD}})\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{i}^{\prime}})=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{DD}})\otimes\underbrace{\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{e}}\sum\nolimits_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{i}^{\prime}})}_{=:\rho^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:psiDD.rhoD}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\rho_{D}\otimes\rho^{\prime}.

This shows the
\Longrightarrow
-direction.

“
\boldsymbol{\Longleftarrow}
”:

We next show that if
\rho=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\rho_{D}
,
then
\rho\vDash\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
.
Let

\rho^{\circ}:=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{DD}})\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{e}}\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{U}}
for some arbitrary
mixed memories
\sigma_{\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{e}},\sigma_{\mathbf{U}}

of trace
1

on
\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{e}

and
\mathbf{U}
,
respectively. The
\rho^{\circ}

is
(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U})
-separable
and

\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\operatorname{span}\{{\psi_{DD}}\}\otimes\ell^{2}\bigl[{({\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}})^{\complement}}\bigr]=\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
. Furthermore,

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho^{\prime}\otimes\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{DD}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:psiDD.rhoD}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\rho^{\prime}\otimes\rho_{D}
.
This shows the
\Longleftarrow
-direction.

\Box

We will see examples of this predicate in the
rule
Sample
for
sampling statements (
{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D
), and in our analysis of the quantum one-time-pad in

Section 8.3
.

5.2
Separable variables

A concept specific to the quantum setting is for a variable to be
separable, i.e., not entangled with any other variables. (But a
separable variable may be probabilistically correlated!)

Expressing that a variable
{\mathbf{x}}

is separable seems, at the first glance, impossible to do using
predicates (that are modeled as subspaces): Such a predicate would
have to contain, e.g., the states
\lvert{00}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}

and
\lvert{11}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}

(since in both cases,
{\mathbf{x}}

and
{\mathbf{y}}

are equal but not entangled) but not the state

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{00}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{11}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}
.
But that would mean that the predicate is not closed under linear
combinations, hence not a subspace.

Yet, by introducing ghosts, we can model separable variables.
To understand how, we first need to recall a concept from

[
19
]
, namely the quantum equality (between two variables):

Definition 3
(Quantum equality
[
19
, Defs.
,
]
)

Let
\mathbf{W},\mathbf{W}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{V}

be disjoint lists of quantum variables. (
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
have the
same type.) Let
\mathsf{SWAP}

be the unitary that swaps the content of
\mathbf{W}

and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
.
That is,

\mathsf{SWAP}\bigl({\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}}\otimes\lvert{j}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\otimes\psi^{\prime\prime}}\bigr):=\lvert{j}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}}\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\otimes\psi^{\prime\prime}
for all
i,j\in T

and all quantum memories
\psi^{\prime\prime}
over
\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
.

Then
\mathbf{W}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

is the set of all quantum memories
\psi

on
\mathbf{V}

such that
\mathsf{SWAP}\psi=\psi
.
14
14

14

The
original definition of
\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}

is more general because we can write something like

U{\mathbf{x}}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}U^{\prime}{\mathbf{y}}

meaning that
{\mathbf{x}}

and
{\mathbf{y}}

are equal up to operations
U,V
.
Since we will no explicitly make use of this in this paper, we
only gave the definition of the special case here. But the more
general definition is, of course, also admissible in predicates as
defined here.

(
[
19
]
also presents a number of useful lemmas for rewriting and
simplifying predicates involving
\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}
.)

In other words, we consider
\mathbf{W}

and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

to have equal content (
\mathbf{W}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
)
iff a state is invariant under swapping
\mathbf{W}

and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
.
Now, it turns out that if
\mathbf{W}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
,
but
\mathbf{W}

and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

are not entangled with each other, then
\mathbf{W}

and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

also cannot be entangled with any other variables:

Lemma 3
(Quantum equality & separable states
[
19
, Coro.
]
)

Fix quantum memories
\psi

over
\mathbf{V}\supseteq\mathbf{W}

and
\psi^{\prime}

over
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\supseteq\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
.
Then
\psi\otimes\psi^{\prime}\in(\mathbf{W}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{W}^{\prime})

iff
\psi

and
\psi^{\prime}

are of the form
\psi=\psi_{\mathbf{W}}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W}}

and
\psi^{\prime}=\psi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\otimes\psi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\setminus\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

and
\psi_{\mathbf{W}}=\psi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

15
15

15

Up to renaming of variables, formally

\psi_{\mathbf{W}}=U_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{W}}\,\psi^{\prime}_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

for some quantum memories

\psi_{\mathbf{W}},\psi_{\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W}},\psi_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}},\psi_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\setminus\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}

over
\mathbf{W},\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W},\mathbf{W}^{\prime},\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\setminus\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
, respectively.

But this means that a program variable
{\mathbf{x}}

is separable iff
{\mathbf{x}}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{u}

for some unentangled ghost! (Remember from

Definition 1
that an unentangled ghost
\mathbf{u}

will, by definition, not be entangled with
{\mathbf{x}}
)
Thus we can introduce the following syntactic sugar for predicates:

•

\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
(
\mathbf{X}
is separable).

\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})

is short for
\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{u}

where
\mathbf{u}

is a fresh unentangled ghost (i.e., one that does not occur
elsewhere in the predicate we are formulating) of the same type as
\mathbf{X}
.

The following lemma formalizes our informal reasoning above,

\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
indeed characterizes separability:

Lemma 4
(Separability predicates)

Let
\rho

be a mixed memory on
\mathbf{Y}
.
Let
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{Y}
. Then the following are equivalent:

•

\rho\vDash\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
.

•

\rho
is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X})
-separable.

Proof.
\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})

is syntactic sugar for
\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{u}

and thus is a predicate over some variables
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}

with
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}
.
And
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{u}
have the same type.

“
\boldsymbol{\Longrightarrow}
”:

First we show that if
\rho\vDash\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
,
then
\rho

is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X})
-separable.
Since
\rho\vDash\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
,
there exists a
(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U})
-separable

\rho^{\circ}

with
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq{\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})}

and
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho
.
Thus
\rho^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{U},i}})

for some
\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i},\psi_{\mathbf{U},i}

over
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}

and
\mathbf{U}
, respectively. Then

\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{U},i}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})=({\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{u}}).

By
Lemma 3
, this implies that

\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}=\psi_{\mathbf{X},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}

for some
\psi_{\mathbf{X},i},\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}

over
\mathbf{X}

and
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{X}
,
respectively. Thus
\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}})

is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{X})
-separable.
Thus
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}})
is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X})
-separable.
Hence

\rho=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}})

is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X})
-separable
as well. This shows the
\Longrightarrow
-direction.

“
\boldsymbol{\Longleftarrow}
”:

We next show that if
\rho

is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X})
-separable,
then
\rho\vDash\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
.
Since
\rho

is
(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X})
-separable,

\rho=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{X},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}})

for some quantum memories
\psi_{\mathbf{X},i},\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}

over
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X}
, respectively. Without loss of generality,
\lVert{\psi_{\mathbf{X},i}}\rVert=1
.
Let

\psi^{\circ}_{i}:=\psi_{\mathbf{X},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{E},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{u},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u},i}

where
\psi_{\mathbf{E},i},\psi_{\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u},i}

are arbitrary quantum memories of norm
1

on
\mathbf{E}

and
\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u}
,
respectively, and
\psi_{\mathbf{u},i}:=\psi_{\mathbf{X},i}

except that
\psi_{\mathbf{u},i}

is a quantum memory over
\mathbf{u}

and not over
\mathbf{X}
.
By
Lemma 3
,

\psi_{i}^{\circ}\in({\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{u}})
.
Let
\rho^{\circ}:=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{i}^{\circ}})
.
Then
\rho^{\circ}

is
(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U})
-separable
and

\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}=\operatorname{span}\{\psi_{i}^{\circ}\}_{i}\subseteq({\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{u}})=\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
. And

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{X},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}})=\rho.

(Using that
\psi_{\mathbf{E},i},\psi_{\mathbf{u},i},\psi_{\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u},i}
all have norm
1
.)
Hence
\rho\vDash\mathbf{separable}({\mathbf{X}})
.
This shows the
\Longleftarrow
-direction.

\Box

As an example for the relationship between different predicates,
notice that
\mathbf{distrib}({{\mathbf{x}},D})

implies
\mathbf{separable}({{\mathbf{x}}})

since
\mathbf{distrib}({{\mathbf{x}},D})

implies that
{\mathbf{x}}

is distributed independently from all other variables
(
Lemma 2
). This also follows within our logic by an
application of the
rule
Transmute
below,
see the example after
rule
Transmute
.

5.3
Classical variables

A third application of ghost variables is to formulate predicates
that imply that a variable has a classical state. We say a mixed
memory
\rho

over
\mathbf{Y}

is classical in
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{Y}

iff it is of the form
\rho=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}})\otimes\rho_{i}

for some mixed memories
\rho_{i}

over
\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X}
.
(This is often called a
cq-state
.)

Expressing that a variable
{\mathbf{x}}

is classical seems, at the first glance, impossible to do using
predicates (that are modeled as subspaces): Such a predicate would
have to contain, e.g., the states
\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}

and
\lvert{1}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}

(since those are classical) but not the state

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{1}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}
.
But that would mean that the predicate is not closed under linear
combinations, hence not a subspace.

Yet, by introducing ghosts, we can model classicality. In
order to see how, we introduce a different equality notion between
quantum variables,
\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}
.
Intuitively, two variables are classically equal iff measuring both in
the
computational
basis will always give the same outcome. (So,

\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}

and
\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{y}}}

would be classically equal, but
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{1}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}}

and
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{0}\rangle_{{\mathbf{y}}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{1}\rangle_{{\mathbf{y}}}

would not be.
16
16

16

Somewhat counterintuitively,
{\mathbf{x}}

and
{\mathbf{y}}

are also classically equal if they are in the entangled state

\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{00}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\lvert{11}\rangle_{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}
.
But this will not matter in our setting since we will apply
\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}
only
to unentangled variables.
)
Formally:

Definition 4
(Classical equality)

Let
\mathbf{W},\mathbf{W}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{V}

be disjoint lists of quantum variables. (
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
have the
same type
T
.)

Then
\mathbf{W}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}

is the span of all quantum memories of the form

\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}}\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}^{\prime}}\otimes\psi

with
i\in T

and
\psi
a quantum memory on
\mathbf{V}\setminus\mathbf{W}\mathbf{W}^{\prime}
.

If we think of two variables
{\mathbf{x}},\mathbf{u}

both having the same state
\psi
,
then
{\mathbf{x}}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}

holds if
\psi=\lvert{i}\rangle

for some
i

(i.e., if
\psi

is a classical state). But if
\psi

is a superposition of different
\lvert{i}\rangle
,
then measuring both
{\mathbf{x}}

and
\mathbf{u}

in the computational basis gives different results with non-zero
probability. Hence
{\mathbf{x}}\not\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}

in that case. This suggests that
{\mathbf{x}}

is classical iff it is classically equal to some unentangled ghost
variable
\mathbf{u}
.
17
17

17

Classical
equality to some entangled ghost would not be sufficient due to the
situation described in
footnote 16
.
That is,
we introduce the following syntactic sugar for predicates:

•

\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
(
\mathbf{X}
is classical).

\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})

is short for
\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}

where
\mathbf{u}

is a fresh unentangled ghost (i.e., one that does not occur
elsewhere in the predicate we are formulating) of the same type as
\mathbf{X}
.

The following lemma formalizes our informal reasoning above,

\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
indeed characterizes classicality:

Lemma 5
(Classicality predicates)

Let
\rho

be a mixed memory over
\mathbf{Y}
.
Let
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{Y}
. Then the following are equivalent:

•

\rho\vDash\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
.

•

\rho
is classical in
\mathbf{X}
. (As defined at the beginning of this section.)

Proof.
\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})

is syntactic sugar for
\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}

and thus is a predicate over some variables
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}

with
\mathbf{X}\subseteq\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{U}
.

“
\boldsymbol{\Longrightarrow}
”:

First we show that if
\rho\vDash\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
,
then
\rho

is classical in
\mathbf{X}
.
Since
\rho\vDash\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
,
there exists a
(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U})
-separable

\rho^{\circ}

with
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})}
and
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\rho
.
Thus
\rho^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{U},i}})

for some
\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i},\psi_{\mathbf{U},i}

over
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}

and
\mathbf{U}
,
respectively. Without loss of generality,
\lVert{\psi_{\mathbf{U},i}}\rVert\neq 0
for all
i
.
And

\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{U},i}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
.
Fix some
i
.
(We will omit
i

from the subscripts for now.) We can write
\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}}

and
\psi_{\mathbf{U}}

as

\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\lvert{j}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{X},j}
and

\psi_{\mathbf{U}}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}^{\prime}\lvert{j}\rangle_{\mathbf{u}}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u},j}
. Since

\psi_{\mathbf{U}}\neq 0
,
we have
\lambda_{j^{*}}^{\prime}\neq 0

for some
j^{*}
.
If
\lambda_{j}\neq 0

for some
j\neq j^{*}
,
then
\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}}\otimes\psi_{\mathbf{U}}

is not in the span of states

\lvert{\nu}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\lvert{\nu}\rangle_{\mathbf{u}}\otimes\dots

and thus not in
\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})=(\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u})
.
Hence
\lambda_{j}\neq 0

for all
j\neq j^{*}
.
Thus

\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}}=\lvert{j^{*}}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\lambda_{j^{*}}\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}\setminus\mathbf{X},j^{*}}
.
Hence
\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}}})

is classical in
\mathbf{X}
. Hence
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E}}})
is classical in
\mathbf{X}
.
Now we “unfix”
i
.
Thus all
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}})
are classical in
\mathbf{X}
. Hence

\rho=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},i}})

is classical in
\mathbf{X}
. This shows the
\Longrightarrow
-direction.

“
\boldsymbol{\Longleftarrow}
”:

We next show that if
\rho

is classical in
\mathbf{X}

then
\rho\vDash\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
.
Since
\rho

is classical in
\mathbf{X}
,

\rho=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}})\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}})

for some quantum memories
\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}

over
\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X}
.
Let

\rho^{\circ}:=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{X}}})\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}})\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{E}}\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{u}}})\otimes\sigma_{\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u}}

for arbitrary mixed memories

\sigma_{\mathbf{E}},\sigma_{\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u}}

of trace
1

over
\mathbf{E}

and
\mathbf{U}\setminus\mathbf{u}
,
respectively. Each summand has support in

({\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}})=\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
, hence
\operatorname{\mathbf{supp}}\rho^{\circ}\subseteq\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
. And
\rho^{\circ}
is
(\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{E},\mathbf{U})
-separable. Finally,

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{E}\mathbf{U}}\rho^{\circ}=\sum_{i}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})_{\mathbf{X}}\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{X},i}})=\rho
.
Thus
\rho\vDash\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
.
This shows the
\Longleftarrow
-direction.

\Box

The predicate
\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
