\frac{\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\quad\{{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}^{\prime}}\}\mathsf{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{B}\quad}{\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}}\quad\textsc{{Conseq}{\@index{\@indexphrase[Conseq]{{Conseq} (rule)}}}\addcontentsline{rule}{rule}{{Conseq}}
}

Proof.
From
Skip
and
Seq
, we get

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{skip}};\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}};\underline{\mathbf{skip}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.
Since
\underline{\mathbf{skip}}

is the neutral element for
;
(
\autopageref
page:seq.assoc.skip.neutral),
this implies
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.

\Box

7.1
Derived rules for derived language elements

Initialization.

The next rules deal with the initialization of variables. They are
generalizations of
rule
Init
, dealing with the syntactic sugar

\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi

(initialization) and
\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z

(classical initialization).

\frac{}{\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi\,}\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}}

InitQ

\frac{}{\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z\,}\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})}\bigr\}}

InitC

The derivation of
rule
InitQ
is not difficult, but the proof of

rule
InitQ
provides a nice first example of reasoning with
sequences of Hoare judgments. The derivation of
rule
InitC
is a little more
involved, and it gives an example how to use rules
ShapeShift
and
Transmute
to
show that the content of a variable is classical.

Proof of
InitQ
.

\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi

is syntactic sugar for

\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}_{1};\dots;\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}_{n};{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U_{\psi}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}

where
U_{\psi}\lvert{0,\dots,0}\rangle=\psi
and
{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\dots{\mathbf{x}}_{n}:=\mathbf{X}
.
For variables
\mathbf{E}:=\mathbf{e}_{1}\dots\mathbf{e}_{n}
and
\mathbf{e}
of the same type as
\mathbf{X}
, we have:

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}_{1}\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}_{1}/{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\},\ {\mathbf{x}}_{1}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
Init
)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}_{2}\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}_{1}\mathbf{e}_{2}/{\mathbf{x}}_{1}{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}\},\ {\mathbf{x}}_{1}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle,\ {\mathbf{x}}_{2}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
Init
)

\displaystyle\dots\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}_{1}\dots\mathbf{e}_{n-1}/{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\dots{\mathbf{x}}_{n-1}}\},\ {\mathbf{x}}_{1}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle,\dots,{\mathbf{x}}_{n-1}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}}_{n}\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
Init
)

\displaystyle{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U_{\psi}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}\ \bigl\{{({U_{\psi}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle})}\bigr\}

(
rule
Apply
)

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{E}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\Rrightarrow\ \bigl\{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}}\bigr\}

(
rule
Rename
)

Then by
rule
Seq
, we get

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi\,}\{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}}\}
.

\Box

Proof of
rule
InitC
.

Recall that
\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z
is syntactic sugar for
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{z}\rangle
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle\Rrightarrow\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}{\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle\otimes\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
ShapeShift
with
\mathbf{E}:=\varnothing
,
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\Rrightarrow\ \bigl\{{\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({i})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{u}})\cdot({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle\otimes\lvert{z}\rangle})}\bigr)}\bigr\}\hskip-144.54pt

(
rule
Transmute
with
M_{i}:=\mathsf{proj}({i})
,
\mathbf{G}:=\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle\otimes\lvert{z}\rangle}}}\bigr\}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle\otimes\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}}}}\bigr\}\hskip-144.54pt

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}}}}\bigr\}\ =\ \bigl\{{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})}}}\bigr\}\hskip-144.54pt

Here
(*)

follows since

({\mathsf{proj}({i})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{u}})\cdot\bigl({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle\otimes\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)={\bigl({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle\otimes\lvert{z}\rangle}\bigr)}
for
i=z

and
=0

otherwise. And
(**)

follows since

({\mathbf{X}\mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle\otimes\lvert{z}\rangle})\subseteq({\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}})
.

By rules
Seq
and
Conseq
, it follows that

\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z\,}\bigl\{{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})}}\bigr\}
.

\Box

Measurements.

More interesting is the rule for measurements because it actively makes use
of ghosts to record the distribution of outcomes. We first look at the
rule for measurements that forget the outcome (
\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}

instead of
\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
)
because it is a bit simpler, and the underlying ideas a the same:

\frac{}{\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}}\quad\textsc{{MeasureForget}{\@index{\@indexphrase[MeasureForget]{{MeasureForget} (rule)}}}\addcontentsline{rule}{rule}{{MeasureForget}}
}

Here
U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{W}}

is the isometry from
\mathbf{V}

to
\mathbf{V}\mathbf{W}

defined by
U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{W}}\,\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}=\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{W}}
. Thus
U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}

is the operation that “classically copies” (in the computational
basis) the content of
\mathbf{X}

to the fresh entangled ghost
\mathbf{e}
.
(We have
\mathbf{e}\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})

is fresh because otherwise
U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot\mathsf{A}

would not be well-defined since it would contain two
\mathbf{e}
’s.)

In other words,
rule
MeasureForget
says that after measuring

\mathbf{X}
,
the result is simply to get
\mathbf{X}

entangled with a fresh entangled ghost
\mathbf{e}
.
Since
\mathbf{e}

is a ghost, being entangled with it effectively means that
\mathbf{X}

has been measured. (It is a well-known fact in quantum information
that entangling with a subsystem that is not observed any more
effectively measures a state.) Thus, the predicate

U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot\mathsf{A}

encodes the fact that
\mathbf{X}

has been measured. At the same time, this predicate does not forget
about the probabilities of the different measurement outcomes. This is
best illustrated by an example: Let
{\mathbf{x}}

be of type integer and
\psi:=\sqrt{2/3}\lvert{1}\rangle+\sqrt{1/3}\lvert{2}\rangle
.
We have

\{{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\,}\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},{\mathbf{x}}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot({{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi})}\}=\{{{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}U_{\mathsf{copy},{\mathbf{x}}\to\mathbf{e}}\psi}\}=\{{{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi^{\prime}}\}

with
\psi^{\prime}:=\sqrt{2/3}\lvert{11}\rangle+\sqrt{1/3}\lvert{22}\rangle
.
As we see, the postcondition encodes the probabilities of measuring

1

and
2

(namely,
2/3

and
1/3
).
Note that the postcondition
{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi^{\prime}

does not mean that
{\mathbf{x}}

is actually entangled with something. Since
\mathbf{e}

is a ghost, it only means that
{\mathbf{x}}

is in a state that can be seen as a hypothetical entanglement with
some
\mathbf{e}
.
In fact, it is easy to see (
Lemma 2
) that the only
mixed memory on
{\mathbf{x}}

satifying
{\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi^{\prime}

is
\rho=\frac{2}{3}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{1}\rangle})+\frac{1}{3}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{2}\rangle})
,
as expected. Thus the postcondition faithfully encodes the
probabilities of the measurement outcome, something that would not
have been possible without using ghosts.

Since measurements are merely syntactic sugar in our language, it
turns out that
rule
MeasureForget
can be easily derived from the
more basic rules we saw so far:

Proof of
MeasureForget
.

Recall from
\autopageref
page:measuref that
\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
is syntactic sugar for
“
{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle;{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle
”
for some fresh
{\mathbf{z}}
(in particular,
{\mathbf{z}}\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A}),\mathbf{X}
).
We then have for some fresh
\mathbf{e}
:

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/{\mathbf{z}}}\},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}\ =\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}}\ \bigl\{{({\mathsf{CNOT}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}}})\cdot({\mathsf{A},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle})}\bigr\}

(
rule
Apply
)

\displaystyle\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to{\mathbf{z}}}\cdot\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot\mathsf{A},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle\!\subseteq\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}.

Here
(*)
follows since
({\mathsf{CNOT}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}}})(\psi\otimes\lvert{0}\rangle)=U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to{\mathbf{z}}}\psi
.

Then by rules
Seq
and
Conseq
, we get

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot\mathsf{A}}\}
.

\Box

The postcondition of
rule
MeasureForget
encodes both the
distribution of outcomes, as well as the state after the
measurement. Sometimes, it may not be necessary to remember the
distribution (only which outcomes are possible). In this case we can
use the following weaker rule:

\frac{}{\Bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\Bigr\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\Bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}}\Bigr\}}\quad\textsc{{MeasureForget*}{\@index{\@indexphrase[MeasureForget*]{{MeasureForget*} (rule)}}}\addcontentsline{rule}{rule}{{MeasureForget*}}
}

To understand the rule, it is easiest to look at the same example as
above. Recall that
\psi=\sqrt{2/3}\lvert{1}\rangle+\sqrt{1/3}\lvert{2}\rangle

and
{\mathbf{x}}

is of type integer. Then
MeasureForget*
implies

\bigl\{{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee_{i}({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{x}}})\cdot({{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi})}\bigr\}=\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee_{i}{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\psi}}\}.

Since
\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\psi=0

for
i\notin\{1,2\}

and
\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\psi=\lvert{i}\rangle
up to scalar factor
for
i=1,2
, we have that
\bigvee_{i}{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\psi}
equals
{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{1}\rangle\vee{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{2}\rangle
. Thus

\bigl\{{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}{\,\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ {\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{1}\rangle\vee{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{2}\rangle}\bigr\}.

In other words, after measuring
{\mathbf{x}}
,

{\mathbf{x}}
will be classical and have a state
\lvert{1}\rangle
or
\lvert{2}\rangle
.

Rule
MeasureForget*
is derived from
MeasureForget
by
rule
Transmute
:

Proof of
MeasureForget*
.

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}}\cdot\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}

(
rule
MeasureForget
)

\displaystyle\Rrightarrow\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{u}})\cdot({U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{u}}\cdot\mathsf{A}})}\bigr)}\bigr\}\hskip-144.54pt

(
rule
Transmute
with
M_{i}:=\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})
,
\mathbf{G}:=\mathbf{e}
,
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A},\ \mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}\bigr\}\hskip-144.54pt

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle,\ \mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A},\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u}}\bigr)}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u},\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}{({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}{({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}}}\bigr\}.

Here
(*)

follows since

{({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{u}})}{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{u}}}\phi={({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})}\phi\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{u}}
for all
\phi
.
And
(**)
follows since

({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\subseteq({\mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle})
.

\Box

Above, we studied measurements that forget their outcome
(
\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
).
When we consider measurement that remember their outcome
(
\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
),
we get the following analogues to
MeasureForget
and

MeasureForget*
:

\frac{}{\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\}}\bigr\}}

Measure

\frac{}{\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}\bigr\}}

Measure*

As one can see, the only differences to
MeasureForget
and

MeasureForget*
is that the measurement is additionally
written to
\mathbf{Y}

(either via
U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}

or via
\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle
).
And additionally,
\mathbf{Y}

is replaced by
\mathbf{e}

in
\mathsf{A}

if it occurs there because it is overwritten (analogous to

rule
Init
,
rule
InitQ
,
rule
InitC
). For example, with

\psi

and
{\mathbf{x}}

as above, we get

\{{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\}{\,{\mathbf{y}}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\,}\{{{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi^{\prime\prime}}\}

with
\psi^{\prime\prime}:=\sqrt{2/3}\lvert{111}\rangle+\sqrt{1/3}\lvert{222}\rangle

and

\{{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\}{\,{\mathbf{y}}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\,}\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ \mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{y}}}),\ {\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{11}\rangle\vee{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{22}\rangle}\}
. The proofs of
these rules are very similar to those of
MeasureForget
and

MeasureForget*
:

Proof of
Measure
.

Recall from
\autopageref
page:measure that
\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
is syntactic sugar for
“
\mathbf{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle;{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle;{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{Y};{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}};{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle
”
for some fresh
{\mathbf{z}}
(in particular,
{\mathbf{z}}\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A}),\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}
).
We then have for some fresh
\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle\ \bigl\{{\bigl({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr)\{{\mathbf{e}^{\prime}/{\mathbf{z}}}\},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle\!=\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle,\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

\displaystyle({\mathbf{z}}\notin\mathsf{fv}(A),\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{e})

\displaystyle{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}\mathbf{Y}\ \bigl\{{({\mathsf{CNOT}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Y}})\cdot\bigl({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr),\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
Apply
,
{\mathbf{z}}\notin\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Y}
)

\displaystyle\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

\displaystyle{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ \mathsf{CNOT}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}}\ \bigl\{{({\mathsf{CNOT}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}{\mathbf{z}}})\cdot\bigl({U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr)}\bigr\}

(
rule
Apply
)

\displaystyle\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to{\mathbf{z}}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\lvert{0}\rangle\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ {\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle\!\subseteq\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\}}\bigr\}.

Here
(*)

follows since

({\mathsf{CNOT}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Y}})(\psi\otimes\lvert{0}\rangle)=U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\psi

for all
\psi
. And
(**)
analogously.

Then by rules
Seq
and
Conseq
, we get

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\}}\}
.

\Box

Proof of
Measure*
.

We first derive an auxiliary fact:

\bigl({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{Y},\ \mathsf{B}}\bigr)\Rrightarrow\bigl({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ \mathsf{B}}\bigr)\qquad\text{for any $\mathsf{B}$}

(4)

By
rule
Case
with
\mathsf{C}:=\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
,

M:=\{\lvert{z}\rangle\}

and
Lemma 6
(or more simply using the

rule
CaseClassical
we introduce on

\autopageref
rule:CaseClassical below), (
4
) follows from:

\displaystyle({\mathbf{X}=\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{Y},\ \mathsf{B}})\subseteq({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{Y}=\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\Rrightarrow}}({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{Y}=\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{e}=\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathsf{B}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\Rrightarrow}}({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{Y}=\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{u}=\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathsf{B}})

\displaystyle\subseteq({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{Y}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u},\ \mathsf{B}})=({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ \mathsf{B}}).

Here
(*)

follows from
ShapeShift
with
\mathbf{X}:=\mathbf{E}:=\varnothing
,

\mathbf{E}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{e}
,

\psi:=1
,

\psi^{\prime}:=\lvert{z}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}}
.
And
(**)

follows from
Transmute
with
M_{i}=\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})
,

\mathbf{G}:=\mathbf{e}
,

\mathbf{G}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}
, and simplification.

We proceed to the actual proof of
Measure*
. We calculate:

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\}}\bigr\}

(
rule
Measure
)

\displaystyle\Rrightarrow\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{u}}\cdot U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\}}\bigr\}\hskip-144.54pt

(
rule
Transmute
with
M_{i}:=\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})
,
\mathbf{G}:=\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}:=\mathbf{u}
)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\}},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle,\ \mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}\bigr\}\hskip-144.54pt

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle,\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle,\ \mathbf{u}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{\textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle,\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u},\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{Y}}\bigr)}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{u},\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{Y},\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}{\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{X}\equiv_{\mathfrak{c\mkern-1.0mul}}\mathbf{Y},\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}{\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny\eqref{eq:intro.class}}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{\Rrightarrow}}\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{Y}}\},\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr)}\bigr\}

Here
(*)

follows since

{({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{u}})}{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{u}}}{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{Y}}}\phi={({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})}\phi\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{u}}\otimes\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{Y}}
for all
\phi
.
And
(**)
follows since

({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}\subseteq({\mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle})
.
Then
Measure*
follows using
Conseq
and
Seq
.

\Box

Sampling.

Finally, we consider sampling
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D
. Again, we have a
rule
Sample
that remembers the distribution

D
,
and a
rule
Sample*
that only remembers which values can occur.

\frac{}{\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D\,}\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})}\bigr\}}

Sample

\frac{}{\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}{\,\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D\,}\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee_{i\in\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D}\mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}\bigr\}}

Sample*

Here
\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D

is the support of the distribution
D
,
i.e.,
\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D=\{{i:D(i)\neq 0}\}
.
That is, the postcondition from
Sample
says that
X

is distributed according to
D

(
\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
),
while the postcondition from
rule
Sample*
merely says that
\mathbf{X}

is classical and has value
i

(
\mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle
) for some
i\in\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D
.
Both rules can be derived easily using the definition of

\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})
as syntactic sugar and the rules we have derived above:

Proof of
Sample
.

Recall that
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D

is syntactic sugar for “
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi_{D};\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
”
where
\psi_{D}=\sum_{i\in T}\sqrt{D(i)}\lvert{i}\rangle
.
And
\mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})

is syntactic sugar for
\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{DD}

where
{\psi_{DD}}=\sum_{i}\sqrt{D(i)}\lvert{i,i}\rangle
and
\mathbf{e}
is fresh. We have (with fresh
\mathbf{e},\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
):

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi_{D}\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{D}}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\ \bigl\{{U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\cdot({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{D}})}\bigr\}

(
rule
MeasureForget
)

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ U_{\mathsf{copy},\mathbf{X}\to\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\cdot({\mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{D}})}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ {\mathbf{X}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{DD}}}\bigr\}\ =\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{distrib}({\mathbf{X},D})}\bigr\}.\hskip-14.22636pt

Rule
Sample
then follows by
rule
Seq
.

\Box

Proof of
Sample*
.

Recall that
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D

is syntactic sugar for “
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi_{D};\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
”
where
\psi_{D}=\sum_{i\in T}\sqrt{D(i)}\lvert{i}\rangle
.
We have (with fresh
\mathbf{e}
):

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi_{D}\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{D}}\bigr\}

(
rule
InitQ
)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}({\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot({\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{D}})}\bigr\}\hskip-72.26999pt

(
rule
MeasureForget*
)

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}\bigl({{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\psi_{D}}}\bigr)}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i}{{\mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\psi_{D}}}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{\mathsf{A}\{{\mathbf{e}/\mathbf{X}}\},\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee\nolimits_{i\in\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D}{{\mathbf{X}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}}}\bigr\}.

Here
(*)
follows since
\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\psi_{D}=0
for
i\notin\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D
,
and
0\neq\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{i}\rangle})\,\psi_{D}\propto\lvert{i}\rangle
for
i\in\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D
.

Rule
Sample*
then follows by rules
Seq
and
Conseq
.

\Box

Final note.

Without the concept of ghosts, we would not
have been able to express
rule
Init
and thus not have been able
to derive the above rules. Instead, we would have had to directly
prove rules for measurements and sampling directly from the
semantics. And without ghosts, those rules would not have been as
expressive, for example,
Sample
would not have been expressible
(i.e., we cannot express what distribution
\mathbf{X}

has after sampling), and
rule
Sample*
would lack the predicate

\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
,
i.e., we cannot express that
\mathbf{X}

is not a superposition between different
\lvert{i}\rangle
with
i\in\operatorname{\mathrm{supp}}D
.

7.2
Programs with classical variables

In this section, we show how programs using classical variables can be
conveniently treated in our logic, even though the definition of our
programming language does not contain classical variables. The lack of
classical variables in language and logic has, at the first glance,
a number of negative consequences:

(i)

Any classical values in a program need to be encoded as quantum
states. In particular, reasoning steps that hold only for classical
variables cannot be applied. (E.g., a case distinction over the
value of the classical variable.)

(ii)

Quantum operations cannot be parametrized by classical
values. For example, we might wish to model a program step such as

{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U_{{\mathbf{y}}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}
,
i.e.,
U_{i}

is a family of isometries, and the classical variable
{\mathbf{y}}

selects which of them is applied to
{\mathbf{x}}
.
For example, in
[
19
]
, every program step can be parametrized
by all classical variables, and this possibility is essential for
expressing more complicated programs (e.g., the cryptographic
schemes analyzed there).

(iii)

Predicates cannot depend on classical values. For example, we
might wish to say something like
{\mathbf{x}}\in_{\mathfrak{q}}S_{{\mathbf{y}}}
,
i.e.,
S_{i}

is a family of subspaces, and
{\mathbf{x}}

lies in the subspaces
S_{{\mathbf{y}}}

selected by the classical variable
{\mathbf{y}}
.
For example,
[
19
]
handles this by defining predicates to be
families of subspaces indexed by the values of the classical
variables (and not simply subspaces as is the case here). Such
predicates are necessary for more complex analyses, e.g., think of
Grover’s algorithm
[
10
]
where the loop invariant would have
to state that the quantum register is in a state that depends on how
many iterations have been performed so far (the iteration counter
being a classical variable).

As we see, a special treatment of classical variables is almost
essential for convenient reasoning about hybrid programs (i.e.,
programs that contain both classical and quantum values), yet such a
special treatment comes with a large formal overhead (the semantics
are more complex, all proofs need to distinguish between classical and
quantum variables). In this section, we will see how ghost variables
allow us to recover the benefits of classical variables without the
formal overhead, simply by introducing additional syntactic sugar and
some derived rules.

Syntactic sugar for programs.

In our language,
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}

requires
U

to be a constant. Since
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi
,

\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z
,
and
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D

are all syntactic sugar based on
\underline{\mathbf{apply}}
,
they inherit this restriction, i.e.,
\psi,z,D

are constants as well. Thus we cannot even write something as simple
as
{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow{\mathbf{y}}
,
meaning we assign the content of the classical variable
{\mathbf{y}}

to
{\mathbf{x}}
.
We introduce some syntactic sugar for
\underline{\mathbf{apply}}

that solves this problem:

Consider the term
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}

where
U

is an expression containing program variables
\mathbf{Y}

of type
T

(disjoint from
\mathbf{X}
).
For any assignment
z

to the variables
\mathbf{Y}
,

U\{z/\mathbf{Y}\}

defines an isometry
{U}^{[{z}]}

(
U

evaluated for
\mathbf{Y}:=z
).
Let
{U}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}

be the isometry on
\mathbf{Y}\mathbf{X}

defined by

{U}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}(\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{Y}}\otimes\psi):=\lvert{i}\rangle_{\mathbf{Y}}\otimes U_{i}\psi
. (That is
{U}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}

is a controlled operation, like CNOT.) Finally,

{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}

is syntactic sugar for
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ {U}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{Y}\mathbf{X}
.

This notation is best understood by looking at a typical example.
Consider
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}

where
H

is some fixed Hermitian operator, and
{\mathbf{y}}

has type
\mathbb{R}
.
Since
U:=e^{-2\pi i{\mathbf{y}}H}

contains the variable
{\mathbf{y}}
,
it defines the family
{U}^{[{z}]}:=e^{-2\pi izH}
of unitaries.
Then
{U}^{[{{\mathbf{y}}}]}(\lvert{z}\rangle_{\mathbf{Y}}\otimes\psi)=\lvert{z}\rangle_{\mathbf{Y}}\otimes e^{-2\pi izH}\psi
. Then

{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\ =\ {\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ {U}^{[{{\mathbf{y}}}]}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{x}}
applies

{U}^{[{z}]}:=e^{-2\pi izH}
to
{\mathbf{x}}
if
{\mathbf{y}}
is in state
\lvert{z}\rangle
, as expected.

Note that this notation does not require that
\mathbf{Y}

refers to classical variables. It is meaningful to use this notation
when
\mathbf{Y}

does not contain classical data. However, in the remainder of this
paper, we will only use this notation when we think of
\mathbf{Y}

as classical variables.

Since
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\psi
,

\mathbf{X}\leftarrow z
,
and
\mathbf{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}D

are all syntactic sugar based on
\underline{\mathbf{apply}}
,
this notation automatically carries over to those constructs, too. For
example,
{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}{\mathbf{y}}

is syntactic sugar for
\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}};{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ U_{\lvert{{\mathbf{y}}}\rangle}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}

with
U_{\lvert{z}\rangle}\lvert{0}\rangle:=\lvert{z}\rangle

which is syntactic sugar for
\underline{\mathbf{init}}\ {\mathbf{x}};{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ {({U_{\lvert{{\mathbf{y}}}\rangle}})}^{[{{\mathbf{y}}}]}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{x}}

where
{{({U_{\lvert{{\mathbf{y}}}\rangle}})}^{[{{\mathbf{y}}}]}}\lvert{z}\rangle\lvert{0}\rangle=\lvert{z}\rangle\lvert{z}\rangle
.
Hence
{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathfrak{q}}}}}}{{\leftarrow}}{\mathbf{y}}

will initialize
{\mathbf{y}}

with
\lvert{z}\rangle

when
{\mathbf{x}}

contains
\lvert{z}\rangle
,
as expected.
Similarly, had we chosen to define a more complicated measurement
command in
Section 3
(instead of
\mathbf{Y}\leftarrow\underline{\mathbf{measure}}\ \mathbf{X}
) that takes the measurement
basis as an additional argument, then that measurement command would
generalize analogously and allow us specify a basis that depends on
classical variables.

Syntactic sugar for predicates.

We use similar syntactic sugar for writing predicates that depend on
classical variables. Without such syntactic sugar, predicates such as

{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi

can only contain a constant
\psi
,
i.e.,
\psi
cannot depend on classical variables. An expression
\mathsf{A}

containing some (supposedly classical) variables
\mathbf{Y}

of type
T

defines a family
{\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}

(
z\in T
)
of predicates with
\mathsf{fv}({\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]})\cap\mathbf{Y}=\varnothing
,
resulting from substituting
\mathbf{Y}

by
z

in the expression
\mathsf{A}
.
We then define the predicate

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}$}}}:=\bigvee_{z}\bigl({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}}\bigr)
. (That
is,
\lvert{z}\rangle_{\mathbf{Y}}\otimes\psi\in{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}

iff
\psi\in{\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}
.)
We can now use the notation
{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}

in pre-/postconditions to parametrize predicates by the values of
classical variables. In most cases, we omit the
[Y]
,
writing simply the predicate
\mathsf{A}
.
While this notation is potentially ambiguous, in most cases it will be
clear where the
[Y]

has to be added since otherwise the pre-/postconditions will not be
welltyped.

We illustrate this by example: Consider the judgment

\bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{y}}}),\ {\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}{\,{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{y}}}),\ {\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}{e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}}\lvert{0}\rangle}\bigr\}
. We
have seen in the previous paragraph how to read

{{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}}
.
The precondition does not contain any syntactic sugar related to
classical variables. We now translate the postcondition. Without
omission of the implicitly understood
[y]
,
it reads

\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{y}}}),\ {({{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}{e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}}\lvert{0}\rangle})}^{[{{\mathbf{y}}}]}
. (The
[y]

cannot be placed elsewhere since including the
\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{y}}})

in it would be mean that our postcondition contain non-welltyped
subterms terms
\mathbf{class}({z})
for real
z
. And
we cannot have
[xy]
instead of
[y]
since that would lead to non-welltyped subterms

z_{1}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\dots
where
z_{1}
is not a variable.)
Then
{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}{e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}}\lvert{0}\rangle}
defines a family of predicates

{{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}{e^{-2i\pi zH}}\lvert{0}\rangle}
for real
z
, and

{({{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}{e^{-2i\pi{\mathbf{y}}H}}\lvert{0}\rangle})}^{[{{\mathbf{y}}}]}
means

\bigvee_{z}\bigl({{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ {{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}{e^{-2i\pi zH}}\lvert{0}\rangle}}\bigr)
.
Thus the postcondition would, without the syntactic sugar, read

\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{y}}}),\ \bigvee_{z}\bigl({{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ {{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}{e^{-2i\pi zH}}\lvert{0}\rangle}}\bigr)
.
Of course, given appropriate rules such as
ApplyParam
below,
one rarely needs to actually explicitly unfold the syntactic sugar.

Derived rules.

Since the syntactic sugar introduced in this section expands to
language constructs for which we already have introduced rules, we
could, in principle, reason about programs involving classical
variables with only the rules above. However, in practice this may be
cumbersome. Therefore we will now introduce a few derived rules
specifically for the dealing with such programs. The first is a simple
consequence of
rule
Case
:

\frac{\forall z.\ \{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}}{\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}}\quad\textsc{{CaseClassical}{\@index{\@indexphrase[CaseClassical]{{CaseClassical} (rule)}}}\addcontentsline{rule}{rule}{{CaseClassical}}
}

Proof.
Let
T
be the type of
\mathbf{Y}
. For any
z\in T
, we have

({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}})=({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \bigvee_{z^{\prime}}({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z^{\prime}}\rangle,\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z^{\prime}}]}})})=({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}})
. (The first equality follows since

({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z^{\prime}}\rangle,\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z^{\prime}}]}})=0
for
z\neq z^{\prime}
.)
Thus the premise of
CaseClassical
becomes

\forall z.\ \{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.
By
Case
(with
\mathsf{A}:=({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}})
,

\mathsf{C}:=\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
,
M:=\{\lvert{z}\rangle\}_{z\in T}
),
we get

\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.
(Using
Lemma 6
to show
\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{X}})
is

\{\lvert{z}\rangle\}_{z\in T}
-disentangling.)

\Box

From this rule, we can derive a rule for our classically parametrized

\underline{\mathbf{apply}}
-command:

\frac{\text{$\mathsf{fv}(e)\subseteq\mathbf{Y}$}\quad\mathbf{Y}\cap\mathbf{X}=\varnothing}{\bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}\bigr\}{\,{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ e\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\bigl({({e\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}}\bigr)}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}\bigr\}}\quad\textsc{{ApplyParam}{\@index{\@indexphrase[ApplyParam]{{ApplyParam} (rule)}}}\addcontentsline{rule}{rule}{{ApplyParam}}
}

Note that his rule is basically the same as
rule
Apply

(especially if we write it with omitted
[Y]
),
except that we allow expression
e

that specifies the operation to apply to contain variables
\mathbf{Y}

that must be guaranteed to be classical in the precondition
(
\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}})
).

Proof.
Let
\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}:=\mathsf{fv}(e)
. Let
\mathbf{Y}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathbf{Y}\setminus\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}
.
Let
T,T^{\prime},T^{\prime\prime}
be the types of
\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}^{\prime},\mathbf{Y}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.
Then
T=T^{\prime}\times T^{\prime\prime}
(up to a canonical bijection).
Then
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ e\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}

is syntactic sugar for
{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ {e}^{[{\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}}]}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\mathbf{X}

where
{e}^{[{z^{\prime}}]}:=e\{z^{\prime}/\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\}
for
z^{\prime}\in T^{\prime}
. For any
z=(z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime})\in T
, we have:

\displaystyle\bigl\{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle{{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ {e}^{[{\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}}]}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\mathbf{X}}\ \bigl\{{({{e}^{[{\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}}]}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}\mathbf{X}})\cdot({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}})}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{({{e}^{[{z^{\prime}}]}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}})}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ ({{e}^{[{z^{\prime}}]}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot{\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {({({e\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}})}^{[{z}]}}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(**)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ \textstyle\bigvee_{\tilde{z}}\bigl({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{\tilde{z}}\rangle,\ {({({e\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}})}^{[{z}]}}\bigr)}}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {({({e\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}})}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}}\bigr\}.

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {({({e\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}})}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}}\bigr\}.

Here
(*)
follows from the fact that by definition,
{U}^{[{\mathbf{Y}^{\prime}}]}
operates
as
{U}^{[{z}]}
on quantum memories in
\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle
. And
(**)
follows since

({\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{\tilde{z}}\rangle,{\mathsf{A}}^{[{\tilde{z}}]}})=0
for
z\neq\tilde{z}
.

Thus (using
rule
Conseq
and the syntactic sugar for
\underline{\mathbf{apply}}
):

\{{\mathbf{Y}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{z}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {\mathsf{A}}^{[{z}]}}\}{\,{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ e\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ \mathbf{X}\,}\{{{\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {({({e\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}})}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}}\}

By
rule
CaseClassical
(with

\mathsf{B}:=\bigl({\mathbf{class}({\mathbf{Y}}),\ {({({e\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{X}})\cdot\mathsf{A}})}^{[{\mathbf{Y}}]}}\bigr)
),
we get the conclusion of
rule
ApplyParam
.

\Box

For a simple example of using this rule see the correctness of the
quantum one-time pad (
Section 8.1
).

8
Case Study: Quantum One-time Pad

In this section, we give a more advanced example of using Hoare logic
with ghosts. We analyze the
quantum one-time pad
(QOTP,
[
3
,
16
]
),
a simple encryption scheme for quantum data. First, we analyze its
correctness (i.e., the fact that decryption correctly yields the
original plaintext). This is entirely unproblematic and can be done in
most variants of quantum Hoare logic. We include this case as a warm-up
example for reasoning with mixed quantum and classical data. Then we
turn to the security of the QOTP, i.e., the fact that an encrypted
qubit looks like random data if the key is not known.

For reasons
described below (
Section 8.2
), this is hard or impossible with prior variants of
quantum Hoare logic.

It thus shows nicely the power of ghosts.

The QOTP, presented here in its version for single qubits, is very
simple: The key
x=(x_{1},x_{2})

are two uniformly random classical bits. The plaintext
{\mathbf{y}}

is a qubit. To encrypt, we apply the

Pauli-Z
operator

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle Z$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle Z$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle Z$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle Z$}}}:=\tiny\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
iff
x_{1}=1
. Then we apply
the
Pauli-X
operator

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle X$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle X$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle X$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle X$}}}:=\tiny\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
iff
x_{2}=1
.
Or, written more compactly, to encrypt
{\mathbf{y}}
,
we apply
X^{x_{2}}Z^{x_{1}}
to it.

Decryption works by inverting the sequence of operations, i.e., by applying

Z^{x_{1}}X^{x_{2}}
to
{\mathbf{y}}
.

In our language, the QOTP, consisting of key generation, encryption,
and decryption, is expressed as follows:

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\mathsf{Keygen}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\mathsf{Keygen}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Keygen}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Keygen}$}}}\ :=\ {\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}K,\qquad{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\mathsf{Enc}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\mathsf{Enc}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Enc}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Enc}$}}}\ :=\ {\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\qquad{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\mathsf{Dec}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\mathsf{Dec}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Dec}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Dec}$}}}\ :=\ {\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}

Here
{\mathbf{x}}
has type
K:=\{0,1\}^{2}
(the
key space
),
{\mathbf{y}}

has type
M:=\{0,1\}
(the
message space
). In slight abuse of notation, we also
use
K

and
M

for the uniform distributions over
K

and
M
,
respectively. Note that the definitions of
\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec}

make use of the syntactic sugar from
Section 7.2
.

8.1
Correctness of the QOTP

The correctness of the QOTP can be expressed by the following Hoare judgment:

\forall{\mathbf{z}},\psi.\quad\bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};\mathsf{Dec}\,}\bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}

(5)

We introduced an extra variable
{\mathbf{z}}

here to model that even if
{\mathbf{y}}

is entangled with some other system
{\mathbf{z}}
,
decryption correct restores the state of
{\mathbf{y}}

and its entanglement with
{\mathbf{z}}
.
19
19

19

A
more elementary statement would be

\forall\psi.\ \{{{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};\mathsf{Dec}\,}\{{{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\}
.
Alternatively, we could also state a stronger statement
“
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};\mathsf{Dec}\,}\{{\mathsf{A}}\}

for all
\mathsf{A}

with
{\mathbf{x}}\notin\mathsf{fv}(\mathsf{A})
.”
Both can be proven with essentially the same derivation as (
5
).

Using the rules from this paper, the derivation of (
5
) is elementary:

\displaystyle\bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}\

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}K\ \bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi,\ \mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ {\textstyle\bigvee_{i}{\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{i}\rangle}}\bigr\}

(
rule
Sample*
)

\displaystyle\subseteq\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ {\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}

\displaystyle{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ ({X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})\cdot({{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi})}\bigr\}

(
rule
ApplyParam
)

\displaystyle{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ ({Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})\cdot({X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})\cdot({{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi})}\bigr\}\hskip-72.26999pt

(
rule
ApplyParam
)

\displaystyle=\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ ({Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})\cdot({{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi})}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ ({\mathsf{id}\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{y}}})\cdot({{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi})}\bigr\}\ =\ \bigl\{{\mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}}),\ {{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbf{z}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}}\bigr\}

Here
(*)

uses that
ZZ=XX=\mathsf{id}
.
By rules
Seq
and
Conseq
, and the definitions of

\mathsf{Keygen},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec}
, we then get (
5
).

8.2
The Quantum One-time Pad and Other Logics

In this section, we explain why it is hard or even impossible to
analyze the security of the QOTP in existing quantum Hoare
logics (without ghosts).
This section is not
required for understanding the security proof and can be
skipped. However, it illustrates why we need ghosts. Since this
section is about logics from prior work, and it would be beyond the
scope of this section to introduce those logics in more detail, in
this section we assume some familiarity with the logics referenced
here.

Security of the QOTP means that, after encrypting, the variable
{\mathbf{y}}

is indistinguishable from a uniformly random bit. (More precisely, after running
\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}
.)
How can we model/prove this in different Hoare logics?

Quantum Hoare logic with subspace predicates.

Probably the
simplest and most obvious variant of quantum Hoare logic is quantum
Hoare logic with subspaces. Here pre-/postconditions are modeled as
subspaces (a.k.a. sharp predicates, Birkhoff-von Neumann quantum
logic). See the “Recap Hoare logic” paragraph on

\autopageref
page:recap.hoare for additional details. In this logic,
we cannot express that
{\mathbf{y}}

is uniformly distributed. Specifically, any predicate
\mathsf{A}

on
{\mathbf{y}}

that holds when
{\mathbf{y}}

is a uniformly distributed bit has to also hold for any other
distribution! Namely, since
{\mathbf{y}}

can be
\lvert{0}\rangle

or
\lvert{1}\rangle

(or anything else),
\mathsf{A}

needs to contain
\lvert{0}\rangle

and
\lvert{1}\rangle
.
But the only subspace containing both
\lvert{0}\rangle

and
\lvert{1}\rangle

is the full space
\ell^{2}[{{\mathbf{y}}}]=\top
.
Thus, the only postcondition on
{\mathbf{y}}

that would be satisfied by the QOTP encryption is
\top

which would also be satisfied by any insecure encryption scheme. So
we cannot formulate (let alone prove) any judgment in this variant of
quantum Hoare logic that would express the security of the QOTP.

Quantum Hoare logic with expectations.

A more expressive variant of quantum Hoare logic is Hoare logic with
expectations. Here, predicates are quantitative
‘‘expectations’’,
20
20

20

Analogous to the
classical expectations by Kozen
[
14
]
.
that is, for a given
state
\rho
,
satisfaction of a predicate
\mathsf{A}

is not binary (true/false), but a predicate
\rho

is satisfied to a certain degree. (Formally,
\mathsf{A}

is a Hermitian operator and the degree of satisfaction is defined as

\operatorname{tr}\mathsf{A}\rho
.)
And a Hoare judgment
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

means that
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket(\rho)

satisfies
\mathsf{B}

at least as much as
\rho

satisfies
\mathsf{A}
.
Such a logic can be expressed in two ways, either via Hoare triples
(e.g.,
[
20
]
) or equivalently in terms of weakest preconditions

[
7
]
.

We know that the weakest preconditions of a quantum program determines
the denotational semantics of said program
[
7
]
, that is, if

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}
have the same weakest preconditions, then
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket
.
(Where
\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket

is defined as in
Section 3
.) Or equivalently:

\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket

iff for all predicates
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}
,

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}\Leftrightarrow\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.
(This holds only if the predicates
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}

are expectations!)
Thus we could define security of the QOTP by
requiring that
\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}

has the same weakest preconditions as the program
{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M
.
(Where we do not allow predicates to refer to
{\mathbf{x}}

since security only holds when the key is secret. For simplicity, we
will assume that
{\mathbf{y}}
is the only variable left after execution.)
The weakest precondition for
{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M

and postcondition
\mathsf{B}

is
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\mathsf{B}\cdot\mathsf{id}
.
Thus we can define security of the QOTP as follows: The QOTP is secure
iff for all
\mathsf{B}
, the weakest precondition of
\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}

for postcondition
\mathsf{B}

is
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}B\cdot\mathsf{id}
.
Or in terms of Hoare judgments: The QOTP is secure iff for all

\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}

with
\mathsf{A}\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}B\cdot\mathsf{id}
,
we have
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

and for all
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}

with
\mathsf{A}\nleq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}B\cdot\mathsf{id}
,
we do
not
have
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.

This is formally correct (except for the fact that we glossed over the
fact that there are variables beyond
{\mathbf{y}}
),
and security of the one-time pad can be derived due to the
completeness of the calculus from
[
7
]
. However, the
definition is very awkward. In order to prove the security of the
one-time pad, not only do we need to prove that

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

holds for certain
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}

but also that is does
not
hold for certain others. This is
problematic since usually we reason only in terms of judgments that
hold, and not in terms of judgments that do not hold. (Or, in terms of
weakest precondition, we reason about inequalities, not equalities.)
Especially in the presence of partial specifications, proving that
certain judgments do not hold might be very difficult.

What happens if we simply omit the requirement that some judgments do
not hold? I.e., we use the following definition: The QOTP is secure
iff for all
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}

with
\mathsf{A}\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}B\cdot\mathsf{id}
,
we have
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.
As it turns out, this works for the QOTP (we can show that this
condition is equivalent to the original one). However, this is
accidental, and for slight variations of the QOTP, this might not work
any more.

To illustrate this, consider the following slightly artificial variant

\frac{1}{2}
QOTP
of the QOTP: This variant has an encryption algorithm
\mathsf{Enc_{1/2}}

that terminates only with probability
\frac{1}{2}
,
but that works correctly when it terminates. That is

{\mathchoice{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\displaystyle\mathsf{Enc_{1/2}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\textstyle\mathsf{Enc_{1/2}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptstyle\mathsf{Enc_{1/2}}$}}{\hbox{\pagecolor{gray!20}$\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{Enc_{1/2}}$}}}:=T;\mathsf{Enc}

where
T

is a program that does not touch any variables and terminates with
probability
\frac{1}{2}

(i.e.,
\llbracket{T}\rrbracket(\rho)=\frac{1}{2}\rho
,
e.g., implemented as a loop). Analogous to the above, we can say that
the
\frac{1}{2}
QOTP
is secure iff for all
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}

with
\mathsf{A}\leq\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{tr}B\cdot\mathsf{id}
,
we have
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc_{1/2}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
.
(Note that
\frac{1}{2}

was replaced by
\frac{1}{4}
,
since we now compare with the program
T;{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M

which only satisfies these judgments.) But now consider a program

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
that with probability
\frac{1}{2}
runs
\underline{\mathbf{skip}}
,
and with probability
\frac{1}{2}
runs the original QOTP.
21
21

21

Formally,

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\ :=\ {\mathbf{z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M;\underline{\mathbf{if}}\ {\mathbf{z}}\ \underline{\mathbf{then}}\ \mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\ \underline{\mathbf{else}}\ \underline{\mathbf{skip}}
.

This is clearly not a secure encryption scheme (with probability

\frac{1}{2}
the plaintext is leaked by the program
\underline{\mathbf{skip}}
). Yet, we can check that
when
\mathsf{A}\leq\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{tr}B\cdot\mathsf{id}
,
we have
\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}
. Hence the insecure
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
also satisfies our definition of security!
Thus, our security definition of the
\frac{1}{2}
QOTP
does not guarantee any reasonable security.

Summarizing, if we want to analyze the security of the QOTP or
variants in quantum Hoare logic with expectations, it can be done in
principle, but we need complicated definitions (we cannot express the
security as a single judgment but through an infinite family). And we
need to not only prove that judgments hold but also that some
judgments do not hold. (Except in some cases like the QOTP proper
where positive judgments are sufficient. But seeing this needs
additional extra-logical reasoning and does not generalize, e.g., to
the
\frac{1}{2}
QOTP.)
And even if we surmount these difficulties, it is not clear how we can
reason with such families of judgments in a larger context (e.g., if
the security of the QOTP is needed to derive some property of a larger
program).

Quantum relational Hoare logic.

Another variant of Hoare logic that seems particularly suitable for
the analysis of the QOTP is quantum relational Hoare logic (qRHL

[
19
]
). This logic was specifically designed with cryptographic
proofs in mind, inspired by the success of probabilistic relational
Hoare logic
[
2
]
. In qRHL, Hoare judgments apply to pairs of
programs. Very roughly speaking, a judgment such as

\{{\mathsf{A}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,\sim\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

means that, if the memories of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}

jointly satisfy the predicate
\mathsf{A}

before the execution of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}

and
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}
,
they will jointly satisfy
\mathsf{B}

afterwards. For example, if
\mathbf{X}_{1},\mathbf{X}_{2}

are the variables of
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}
,

\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}
,
respectively, then

\{{\mathbf{X}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{X}_{2}}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,\sim\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\,}\{{\mathbf{X}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{X}_{2}}\}

means that for identical initial states,
\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}},\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}

have identical final states. In other words,
\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket
.
And
\{{\top}\}{\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}\,\sim\,\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}\,}\{{\mathbf{X}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathbf{X}_{2}}\}

would mean that the final states are identical, no matter what the
initial states are, i.e.,

\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}.\ \llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{c}}}\rrbracket(\rho)=\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathfrak{d}}}\rrbracket(\rho^{\prime})
.

Thus, at the first glance, it seems very easy to model the security of
the one-time pad. To specify that
{\mathbf{y}}

is uniformly random after execution of the QOTP (no matter what its
initial state was), we simply require that
{\mathbf{y}}
after the QOTP is the same as
{\mathbf{y}}

after
{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M
.
That is, we say the QOTP is secure iff

\{{\top}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,\sim\,{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M\,}\{{{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}\}
.
22
22

22

Or alternatively, we could
define security as

\{{\top}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,\sim\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,}\{{{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}\}

which means that the ciphertexts
{\mathbf{y}}_{1},{\mathbf{y}}_{2}

have the same distribution, no matter what the plaintexts (initial
values of
{\mathbf{y}}_{1},{\mathbf{y}}_{2}
)
are.
The difficulties described here apply in the same way
to that definition.
(Here
{\mathbf{y}}_{1},{\mathbf{y}}_{2}

refer to the
{\mathbf{y}}

from the left/right program, respectively.) Unfortunately, this does
not work. We can show that

\{{\top}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,\sim\,{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M\,}\{{{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}\}

does not hold in qRHL.
Intuitively, the
reason is that after encrypting,
{\mathbf{y}}_{1}
is still correlated with the key
{\mathbf{x}}_{1}
.
This means that it could still be decrypted to the original plaintext,
and therefore qRHL does not consider it equivalent to a uniformly random
{\mathbf{y}}_{2}

(that is independent of any other variables).

To resolve this, we need to erase the key after encrypting. So the
definition becomes: The QOTP is secure iff

\{{\top}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00\,\sim\,{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M\,}\{{{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}\}.

(6)

This judgment is indeed a good definition
for the security of the QOTP. It is not hard to prove that it holds by
explicitly computing the superoperators

\llbracket{\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00}\rrbracket

and
\llbracket{{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M}\rrbracket
,
and then showing (
6
) directly from the semantic definition of
qRHL. In that sense, qRHL is superior to the two Hoare logic variants
above: at least we can state the security of the QOTP concisely. (And
for variants of it such as
\frac{1}{2}
QOTP, similar definitions work.)

Unfortunately, it seems hard (or impossible) to derive
(
6
)
within
the logic. (That is, by an
application of a sequence of reasoning rules.) While we do not have a
proof that (
6
) cannot be derived from the rules
from
[
19
]
, a natural proof would seem to go along the following
lines: First, we show
\{{\top}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,\sim\,{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

for some
\mathsf{B}
,
then we show
\{{\mathsf{B}}\}{\,{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00\,\sim\,\underline{\mathbf{skip}}\,}\{{{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}\}
,
and then we use the qRHL-analogue to
rule
Seq
to conclude
(
6
). Unfortunately, we can show that there exists
no predicate
\mathsf{B}

such that both
\{{\top}\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,\sim\,{\mathbf{y}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}M\,}\{{\mathsf{B}}\}

and
\{{\mathsf{B}}\}{\,{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00\,\sim\,\underline{\mathbf{skip}}\,}\{{{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\equiv_{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathbf{y}}_{2}}\}

are true. Hence this proof approach is doomed.

To summarize, in qRHL, while it is easy to formulate the security of
the QOTP, there are reasons to believe that the security proof is
difficult or even impossible.

8.3
Security of the Quantum One-time Pad

We will now demonstrate how to prove the security of the QOTP using
quantum Hoare logic with ghosts. Security of the QOTP means that,
after encrypting, the variable
{\mathbf{y}}

is indistinguishable from a uniformly random bit, as long as the key
is not known. We formalize this by requiring that after key
generation, encryption, and subsequent deletion of the key,
{\mathbf{y}}

is uniformly random. As a Hoare judgment, we write this as:

\bigl\{{\top}\bigr\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}})}\bigr\}.

(7)

The
{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00

overwrites the key
{\mathbf{x}}

and is added to model the fact that we do not know the
key.
23
23

23

One might think that it should be sufficient to simply not
mention the key in the postcondition. I.e., to define security
as
\bigl\{{\top}\bigr\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}})}\bigr\}
.
However, from
Lemma 2
we know that in a state
satisfying
\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}})
,
{\mathbf{y}}
is uniform
and independent
of
all other variables.
This is clearly not the case after encryption (
{\mathbf{y}}
is not independent of the key
{\mathbf{x}}
).
Thus
\bigl\{{\top}\bigr\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc}\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}})}\bigr\}
does not hold.

The precondition is
\top
since we do not want to make any assumption
about the initial state of
{\mathbf{y}}
, i.e., about the plaintext.
(In particular, the plaintext can be entangled with other variables.)

Warm up.

Before we show (
7
),
we prove a weaker claim as a warm up:

\forall\psi\neq 0.\quad\bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00\,}\bigl\{{\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}})}\bigr\}.

(8)

This equation say that the QOTP is secure as long as the plaintext

{\mathbf{y}}

is some (arbitraty) state
\psi
.
That is, it only guarantees security for unentangled plaintexts.

We will do the general case (
7
) below,
but the simplified case is simpler and contains already many of the needed ideas.

Recall that
\mathsf{Keygen}\ =\ {\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}K

and
\mathsf{Enc}\ =\ {\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}
. We first
derive a postcondition for
\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00
by simply
applying the reasoning rules step by step:

\displaystyle\bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}\ {\mathbf{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle{}\smash{\$}}}}{{\leftarrow}}K\ \bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi,\ \mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{x}}})}\bigr\}

(
rule
Sample
)

\displaystyle\quad{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}\ \bigl\{{\bigl({{({X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}})}^{[{{\mathbf{x}}}]}\,\mathbf{on}\,{\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}}\bigr)\cdot\bigl({{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi,\ \mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{x}}})}\bigr)}\bigr\}

(
rule
Apply
)

\displaystyle\quad{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00\ \bigl\{{\bigl({{({X^{\mathbf{e}_{2}}Z^{\mathbf{e}_{1}}})}^{[{\mathbf{e}}]}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{e}{\mathbf{y}}}\bigr)\cdot\bigl({{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi,\ \mathbf{uniform}({\mathbf{e}})}\bigr),\ {\mathbf{x}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\lvert{00}\rangle,\ \mathbf{class}({{\mathbf{x}}})}\bigr\}\hskip-72.26999pt

(
rule
InitC
)

\displaystyle\quad\mathord{\subseteq}\ \bigl\{{\bigl({{({X^{\mathbf{e}_{2}}Z^{\mathbf{e}_{1}}})}^{[{\mathbf{e}}]}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{e}{\mathbf{y}}}\bigr)\cdot\bigl({{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi,\ \mathbf{uniform}({\mathbf{e}})}\bigr)}\bigr\}

For the application of
rule
Sample
, recall that

\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{x}}})
is syntactic sugar for
\mathbf{distrib}({{\mathbf{x}},K})
where
K
is the
uniform distribution on the type of
{\mathbf{x}}
.
For the application of
rule
Apply
, recall that

{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{y}}
is syntactic sugar for

{\underline{\mathbf{apply}}}\ {({X^{{\mathbf{x}}_{2}}Z^{{\mathbf{x}}_{1}}})}^{[{{\mathbf{x}}}]}\ \underline{\mathbf{to}}\ {\mathbf{x}}{\mathbf{y}}

(
\autopageref
page:applysugar).
By rules
Seq
and
Conseq
, we immediately get

\bigl\{{{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi}\bigr\}{\,\mathsf{Keygen};\mathsf{Enc};{\mathbf{x}}\leftarrow 00\,}\bigl\{{\bigl({{({X^{\mathbf{e}_{2}}Z^{\mathbf{e}_{1}}})}^{[{\mathbf{e}}]}\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{e}{\mathbf{y}}}\bigr)\cdot\bigl({{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi,\ \mathbf{uniform}({\mathbf{e}})}\bigr)}\bigr\}=:\{\mathsf{B}\}.

(9)

While this is not yet the final result (
8
) we
wanted, we see that the application of the
InitC
rule
already achieved one important thing: Since
{\mathbf{x}}

was turned into a ghost, the postcondition
\mathsf{B}

refers only to the ciphertext
{\mathbf{y}}

and not other variables, i.e., we got rid of the dependence between

{\mathbf{y}}

and
{\mathbf{x}}
.
What is left to do is to prove that the postcondition
\mathsf{B}

implies
\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}})
.

Analyzing
\mathsf{B}

involves some calculations. (This is to be expected because the QOTP
relies on the properties of the involved matrices, so we have to
calculate somewhere.) We first unfold the syntactic sugar.

\mathbf{uniform}({\mathbf{e}})
means
\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{KK}
for some fresh
\mathbf{e}^{\prime}
. Thus

({{\mathbf{y}}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi,\ \mathbf{uniform}({\mathbf{e}})})=({{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi\otimes\psi_{KK}})
. Furthermore

{({X^{\mathbf{e}_{2}}Z^{\mathbf{e}_{1}}})}^{[{\mathbf{e}}]}=\sum_{k\in K}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{k}\rangle})\otimes{X^{k_{2}}Z^{k_{1}}}
(see
\autopageref
page:applysugar).
Thus

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}

\displaystyle={\biggl({\Bigl({\sum_{k\in K}\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{k}\rangle})\otimes{X^{k_{2}}Z^{k_{1}}}}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{e}{\mathbf{y}}}\biggr)\cdot\bigl({{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi\otimes\psi_{KK}}\bigr)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hskip-72.26999pt\text{\tiny$(*)$}\hskip-72.26999pt}}{{=}}\biggl({{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\underbrace{\Bigl({\sum\nolimits_{k}{X^{k_{2}}Z^{k_{1}}}\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{k}\rangle})\otimes\mathsf{id}}\Bigr)({\psi\otimes\psi_{KK}})}_{{}=:\phi}}\biggr).

(Note that in
(*)
,
the tensor product factors in the sum are written in a different order because
the
({\dots\,\mathbf{on}\,\mathbf{e}{\mathbf{y}}})
-term
and the
({\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\dots)
-term
list the variables in a different order.) Since
\mathsf{B}

is now of the form
{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=\phi
,
it is amenable to rewriting using
rule
ShapeShift
. Furthermore,
our intended postcondition
\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}})

is syntactic sugar for
{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{MM}
,
which is also compatible with
ShapeShift
. Specifically, if
we can show
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\mathsf{proj}({\phi})=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{MM}})
,
then
rule
ShapeShift
implies

\mathsf{B}=({{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\phi})\Rrightarrow({{\mathbf{y}}\mathbf{e}=_{\mathfrak{q}}\psi_{MM}})=\mathbf{uniform}({{\mathbf{y}}}).

(10)

(Recall that
M
is the uniform distribution on the type of
{\mathbf{y}}
,
i.e., on
\{0,1\}
.)
We now show
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\mathsf{proj}({\phi})=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}^{\prime\prime}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi_{MM}})

by computation.
Since
\psi_{KK}=\sum_{l\in K}\frac{1}{2}\lvert{l}\rangle\otimes\lvert{l}\rangle
by
definition, we have:

\phi={\sum_{kl}\tfrac{1}{2}{X^{k_{2}}Z^{k_{1}}}\psi\otimes\mathsf{proj}({\lvert{k}\rangle})\,\lvert{l}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}}\otimes\lvert{l}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}}={\sum_{k}\tfrac{1}{2}{X^{k_{2}}Z^{k_{1}}}\psi\otimes\lvert{k}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}}\otimes\lvert{k}\rangle_{\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}}.

Thus

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\mathsf{proj}({\phi})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathbf{e}\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}\sum_{kk^{\prime}}\tfrac{1}{4}{X^{k_{2}}Z^{k_{1}}}\mathsf{proj}({\psi})({X^{k^{\prime}_{2}}Z^{k^{\prime}_{1}}})^{*}\otimes\lvert{k}\rangle\langle{k^{\prime}}\rvert_{\mathbf{e}}\otimes\lvert{k}\rangle\langle{k^{\prime}}\rvert_{\mathbf{e}^{\prime}}
